\begin{equation}
\boxed{
J_I
=
\frac{
C_{i-1}-C_{i+1}
}{
\dfrac{\Delta r^-}{D^-}
+
\dfrac{\Delta r^+}{D^+}
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-338}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-338}
\end{equation}

The denominator is the sum of the two local diffusion resistances per unit area.

Define the total node-to-node distance

\begin{equation}
\Delta r_{\mathrm{tot}}
=
\Delta r^-+\Delta r^+.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-339}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-339}
\end{equation}

Define an effective diffusivity \textbackslash\{\}(D\_\{\textbackslash\{\}mathrm\{eff\}\}\textbackslash\{\}) by

\begin{equation}
J_I
=
D_{\mathrm{eff}}
\frac{
C_{i-1}-C_{i+1}
}{
\Delta r_{\mathrm{tot}}
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-340}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-340}
\end{equation}

Equate (TRISO-DIS-338) and (TRISO-DIS-340):

\begin{equation}
\frac{D_{\mathrm{eff}}}{\Delta r_{\mathrm{tot}}}
=
\frac1{
\dfrac{\Delta r^-}{D^-}
+
\dfrac{\Delta r^+}{D^+}
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-341}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-341}
\end{equation}

Multiply by \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}Delta r\_\{\textbackslash\{\}mathrm\{tot\}\}\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
D_{\mathrm{eff}}
=
\frac{
\Delta r^-+\Delta r^+
}{
\dfrac{\Delta r^-}{D^-}
+
\dfrac{\Delta r^+}{D^+}
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-342}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-342}
\end{equation}

For equal half-distances,

\begin{equation}
\Delta r^-=\Delta r^+,
\tag{TRISO-DIS-343}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-343}
\end{equation}

the effective diffusivity becomes

\begin{equation}
D_{\mathrm{eff}}
=
\frac{2}{
\dfrac1{D^-}
+
\dfrac1{D^+}
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-344}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-344}
\end{equation}

Thus

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
D_{\mathrm{eff}}
=
\frac{
2D^-D^+
}{
D^-+D^+
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-345}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-345}
\end{equation}

This is the harmonic mean of the adjacent diffusivities.

It appears because diffusion resistances add in series; it is not an arbitrary averaging rule.

\subsubsection*{15.9 Limiting checks}

If

\begin{equation}
D^-=D^+=D,
\tag{TRISO-DIS-346}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-346}
\end{equation}

then (TRISO-DIS-323) becomes

\begin{equation}
C_I
=
\frac{
DC_{i-1}+DC_{i+1}
}{
2D
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-347}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-347}
\end{equation}

Cancel \textbackslash\{\}(D\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
C_I
=
\frac{
C_{i-1}+C_{i+1}
}{2}.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-348}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-348}
\end{equation}

This is the expected linear interpolation for equal diffusivity and equal spacing.

If

\begin{equation}
D^+\ll D^-,
\tag{TRISO-DIS-349}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-349}
\end{equation}

then the low-diffusivity outer material contributes the dominant diffusion resistance in (TRISO-DIS-338).

The interface concentration correspondingly approaches the concentration on the high-diffusivity side only according to the resistance-weighted relation; it is not obtained from an arithmetic diffusivity average.

\subsubsection*{15.10 Accuracy and scope}

The one-sided interface gradients in (TRISO-DIS-313) and (TRISO-DIS-316) are first-order approximations to the limiting interface derivatives when used with only one neighbouring node on each side.

Therefore the algebraic interface relation derived here is conservative with respect to the approximated flux, but this particular gradient construction is not automatically second-order accurate at the interface.

A higher-order interface treatment would require additional same-material nodes or a finite-volume formulation with carefully defined face fluxes.

[IMPORTANT] The present result establishes the correct \textbf{discrete transmission logic}:

\noindent\textbullet\ one ideal-interface concentration;\\
\noindent\textbullet\ no differentiation of \textbackslash\{\}(D\textbackslash\{\}) through its jump;\\
\noindent\textbullet\ equal discrete flux on both sides;\\
\noindent\textbullet\ resistance-weighted, rather than arithmetic, diffusivity coupling.\\

It does not yet establish the globally preferred five-layer spatial discretisation.

\subsection*{16. Outer Robin boundary: ghost-point elimination and surface update}

The outer surface is

\begin{equation}
r_N=R.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-400}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-400}
\end{equation}

For the homogeneous Part-I FTCS benchmark, the outer material has constant diffusivity \textbackslash\{\}(D\textbackslash\{\}) and the external bulk concentration is

\begin{equation}
c_\infty=0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-401}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-401}
\end{equation}

The continuous Robin condition is therefore

\begin{equation}
-D
\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}(R,t)
=
h c(R,t).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-402}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-402}
\end{equation}

The left side is the outward diffusive flux from the particle. The right side is the outward external mass-transfer flux.

\subsubsection*{16.1 Introduce the outer ghost point}

The last physical node is

\begin{equation}
r_N=R.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-403}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-403}
\end{equation}

The adjacent physical interior node is

\begin{equation}
r_{N-1}=R-\Delta r.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-404}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-404}
\end{equation}

Introduce a mathematical ghost point outside the particle:

\begin{equation}
r_{N+1}=R+\Delta r.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-405}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-405}
\end{equation}

Its numerical value is denoted

\begin{equation}
C_{N+1}^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-406}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-406}
\end{equation}

The ghost value is not an external physical concentration. It is an algebraic device used to retain a centred derivative at \textbackslash\{\}(r=R\textbackslash\{\}).

\subsubsection*{16.2 Centred approximation of the surface gradient}

At time \textbackslash\{\}(t\_j\textbackslash\{\}), approximate the radial derivative by

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}(R,t_j)
\approx
\frac{
C_{N+1}^j-C_{N-1}^j
}{
2\Delta r
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-407}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-407}
\end{equation}

Substitute this approximation into the Robin condition:

\begin{equation}
-D
\frac{
C_{N+1}^j-C_{N-1}^j
}{
2\Delta r
}
=
hC_N^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-408}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-408}
\end{equation}

Multiply both sides by \textbackslash\{\}(2\textbackslash\{\}Delta r\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
-D
\left(
C_{N+1}^j-C_{N-1}^j
\right)
=
2h\Delta r\,C_N^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-409}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-409}
\end{equation}

Divide by \textbackslash\{\}(-D\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
C_{N+1}^j-C_{N-1}^j
=
-\frac{2h\Delta r}{D}C_N^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-410}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-410}
\end{equation}

Add \textbackslash\{\}(C\_\{N-1\}\textasciicircum{}j\textbackslash\{\}) to both sides:

\begin{equation}
C_{N+1}^j
=
C_{N-1}^j
-
\frac{2h\Delta r}{D}C_N^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-411}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-411}
\end{equation}

Define the dimensionless mesh transfer parameter

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\kappa
=
\frac{h\Delta r}{D}.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-412}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-412}
\end{equation}

Its units are

\begin{equation}
[\kappa]
=
\frac{
\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}\mathrm m
}{
\mathrm{m^2\,s^{-1}}
}
=
1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-413}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-413}
\end{equation}

Therefore the ghost relation is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
C_{N+1}^j
=
C_{N-1}^j
-
2\kappa C_N^j.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-414}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-414}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection*{16.3 Surface approximation of the second radial derivative}

Use the centred second derivative at node \textbackslash\{\}(N\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial^2c}{\partial r^2}(R,t_j)
\approx
\frac{
C_{N-1}^j
-
2C_N^j
+
C_{N+1}^j
}{
\Delta r^2
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-415}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-415}
\end{equation}

Substitute the ghost relation (TRISO-DIS-414):

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial^2c}{\partial r^2}(R,t_j)
\approx
\frac{
C_{N-1}^j
-
2C_N^j
+
C_{N-1}^j
-
2\kappa C_N^j
}{
\Delta r^2
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-416}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-416}
\end{equation}

Collect the two interior-neighbour terms:

\begin{equation}
C_{N-1}^j+C_{N-1}^j
=
2C_{N-1}^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-417}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-417}
\end{equation}

Collect the two surface terms:

\begin{equation}
-2C_N^j-2\kappa C_N^j
=
-2(1+\kappa)C_N^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-418}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-418}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\frac{\partial^2c}{\partial r^2}(R,t_j)
\approx
\frac{
2C_{N-1}^j
-
2(1+\kappa)C_N^j
}{
\Delta r^2
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-419}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-419}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection*{16.4 Surface approximation of the first radial derivative}

The centred derivative is

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}(R,t_j)
\approx
\frac{
C_{N+1}^j-C_{N-1}^j
}{
2\Delta r
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-420}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-420}
\end{equation}

Substitute (TRISO-DIS-414):

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}(R,t_j)
\approx
\frac{
C_{N-1}^j
-
2\kappa C_N^j
-
C_{N-1}^j
}{
2\Delta r
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-421}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-421}
\end{equation}

Cancel the two \textbackslash\{\}(C\_\{N-1\}\textasciicircum{}j\textbackslash\{\}) terms:

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}(R,t_j)
\approx
-\frac{
2\kappa C_N^j
}{
2\Delta r
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-422}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-422}
\end{equation}

Cancel the factor \textbackslash\{\}(2\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}(R,t_j)
\approx
-\frac{\kappa}{\Delta r}C_N^j.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-423}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-423}
\end{equation}

Using \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}kappa=h\textbackslash\{\}Delta r/D\textbackslash\{\}),

\begin{equation}
-\frac{\kappa}{\Delta r}C_N^j
=
-\frac hD C_N^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-424}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-424}
\end{equation}

Thus the eliminated ghost relation reproduces the discrete Robin gradient exactly within the chosen centred boundary approximation.

\subsubsection*{16.5 Discrete spherical operator at the outer surface}

The spherical operator is

\begin{equation}
\mathcal L[c](R,t)
=
c_{rr}(R,t)
+
\frac2R c_r(R,t).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-425}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-425}
\end{equation}

Substitute the discrete second derivative (TRISO-DIS-419):

\begin{equation}
\mathcal L[c](R,t_j)
\approx
\frac{
2C_{N-1}^j
-
2(1+\kappa)C_N^j
}{
\Delta r^2
}
+
\frac2R c_r(R,t_j).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-426}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-426}
\end{equation}

Substitute the discrete first derivative (TRISO-DIS-423):

\begin{equation}
\mathcal L[c](R,t_j)
\approx
\frac{
2C_{N-1}^j
-
2(1+\kappa)C_N^j
}{
\Delta r^2
}
-
\frac{2\kappa}{R\Delta r}C_N^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-427}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-427}
\end{equation}

Use

\begin{equation}
R=N\Delta r.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-428}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-428}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
R\Delta r
=
N\Delta r^2.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-429}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-429}
\end{equation}

Hence

\begin{equation}
\frac{2\kappa}{R\Delta r}
=
\frac{2\kappa}{N\Delta r^2}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-430}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-430}
\end{equation}

Substitute:

\begin{equation}
\mathcal L[c](R,t_j)
\approx
\frac{
2C_{N-1}^j
-
2(1+\kappa)C_N^j
}{
\Delta r^2
}
-
\frac{
2\kappa C_N^j
}{
N\Delta r^2
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-431}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-431}
\end{equation}

Put the terms over the common denominator:

\begin{equation}
\mathcal L[c](R,t_j)
\approx
\frac2{\Delta r^2}
\left[
C_{N-1}^j
-
(1+\kappa)C_N^j
-
\frac{\kappa}{N}C_N^j
\right].
\tag{TRISO-DIS-432}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-432}
\end{equation}

Collect the surface coefficient:

\begin{equation}
(1+\kappa)
+
\frac{\kappa}{N}
=
1+\kappa
\left(
1+\frac1N
\right).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-433}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-433}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathcal L[c](R,t_j)
\approx
\frac2{\Delta r^2}
\left[
C_{N-1}^j
-
\left(
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right)
C_N^j
\right].
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-434}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-434}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection*{16.6 Apply the surface PDE}

For the homogeneous benchmark,

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial c}{\partial t}(R,t)
=
D\mathcal L[c](R,t)
+
S_R.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-435}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-435}
\end{equation}

For the original homogeneous source benchmark,

\begin{equation}
S_R=S_0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-436}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-436}
\end{equation}

For the physical five-layer kernel-confined source problem, the OPyC source would instead be zero. This distinction must be preserved when the boundary formula is reused outside the Part-I benchmark.

Use forward Euler:

\begin{equation}
\frac{
C_N^{j+1}-C_N^j
}{
\Delta t
}
=
D\mathcal L[c](R,t_j)
+
S_R^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-437}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-437}
\end{equation}

Substitute (TRISO-DIS-434):

\begin{equation}
\frac{
C_N^{j+1}-C_N^j
}{
\Delta t
}
=
\frac{2D}{\Delta r^2}
\left[
C_{N-1}^j
-
\left(
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right)
C_N^j
\right]
+
S_R^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-438}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-438}
\end{equation}

Multiply by \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}Delta t\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
C_N^{j+1}-C_N^j
=
\frac{2D\Delta t}{\Delta r^2}
\left[
C_{N-1}^j
-
\left(
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right)
C_N^j
\right]
+
S_R^j\Delta t.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-439}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-439}
\end{equation}

Use

\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Fo}
=
\frac{D\Delta t}{\Delta r^2}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-440}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-440}
\end{equation}

Then

\begin{equation}
C_N^{j+1}-C_N^j
=
2\mathrm{Fo}
\left[
C_{N-1}^j
-
\left(
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right)
C_N^j
\right]
+
S_R^j\Delta t.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-441}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-441}
\end{equation}

Add \textbackslash\{\}(C\_N\textasciicircum{}j\textbackslash\{\}) to both sides:

\begin{equation}
C_N^{j+1}
=
C_N^j
+
2\mathrm{Fo}C_{N-1}^j
-
2\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right)
C_N^j
+
S_R^j\Delta t.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-442}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-442}
\end{equation}

Collect the surface coefficient:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
C_N^{j+1}
=
2\mathrm{Fo}C_{N-1}^j
+
\left[
1
-
2\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right)
\right]
C_N^j
+
S_R^j\Delta t.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-443}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-443}
\end{equation}

This recovers the surface update written in the original Ray derivation.

\subsubsection*{16.7 Dimensional checks}

The Fourier number is dimensionless:

\begin{equation}
[\mathrm{Fo}]=1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-444}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-444}
\end{equation}

The mesh transfer parameter is dimensionless:

\begin{equation}
[\kappa]=1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-445}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-445}
\end{equation}

Therefore every coefficient multiplying a concentration in (TRISO-DIS-443) is dimensionless.

The source increment has units

\begin{equation}
[S_R\Delta t]
=
\mathrm{mol\,m^{-3}}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-446}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-446}
\end{equation}

Thus every term in the update has concentration units.

\subsubsection*{16.8 Limiting checks}

If

\begin{equation}
h=0,
\tag{TRISO-DIS-447}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-447}
\end{equation}

then

\begin{equation}
\kappa=0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-448}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-448}
\end{equation}

The ghost relation becomes

\begin{equation}
C_{N+1}^j=C_{N-1}^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-449}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-449}
\end{equation}

This is the expected symmetric zero-gradient ghost condition for a zero-flux Neumann boundary.

The surface update becomes

\begin{equation}
C_N^{j+1}
=
2\mathrm{Fo}C_{N-1}^j
+
(1-2\mathrm{Fo})C_N^j
+
S_R^j\Delta t.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-450}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-450}
\end{equation}

For finite \textbackslash\{\}(h>0\textbackslash\{\}), increasing \textbackslash\{\}(h\textbackslash\{\}) increases \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}kappa\textbackslash\{\}), which strengthens the outward-transfer contribution in the surface coefficient.

The formal limit \textbackslash\{\}(h\textbackslash\{\}to\textbackslash\{\}infty\textbackslash\{\}) is more delicate for this explicit ghost formulation because

\begin{equation}
\kappa=\frac{h\Delta r}{D}\to\infty
\tag{TRISO-DIS-451}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-451}
\end{equation}

at fixed \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}Delta r\textbackslash\{\}).

The continuum Robin condition approaches the absorbing Dirichlet condition \textbackslash\{\}(c(R,t)=0\textbackslash\{\}), but the explicit ghost update becomes increasingly stiff rather than automatically turning into a numerically well-conditioned Dirichlet update.

Therefore an absorbing Dirichlet boundary should be imposed directly when that is the intended numerical model, rather than obtained by taking \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}kappa\textbackslash\{\}to\textbackslash\{\}infty\textbackslash\{\}) in (TRISO-DIS-443).

\subsubsection*{16.9 Complete truncation error of the Robin ghost surface closure}

The centred Robin derivative by itself is second-order accurate, but the complete surface PDE closure also inserts the ghost value into a second-derivative stencil.

The complete boundary operator must therefore be expanded directly.

Define

\begin{equation}
\beta=\frac{h}{D}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-452}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-452}
\end{equation}

The exact Robin condition gives

\begin{equation}
c_r(R)=-\beta c(R).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-453}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-453}
\end{equation}

The ghost construction is

\begin{equation}
c_g(R+\Delta r)
=
c(R-\Delta r)
-
2\beta\Delta r\,c(R).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-454}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-454}
\end{equation}

Taylor-expand the exact interior value:

\begin{equation}
c(R-\Delta r)
=
c(R)
-
\Delta r\,c_r(R)
+
\frac{\Delta r^2}{2}c_{rr}(R)
-
\frac{\Delta r^3}{6}c_{rrr}(R)
+
O(\Delta r^4).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-455}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-455}
\end{equation}

Substitute the Robin derivative \textbackslash\{\}(c\_r(R)=-\textbackslash\{\}beta c(R)\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
c(R-\Delta r)
=
c(R)
+
\beta\Delta r\,c(R)
+
\frac{\Delta r^2}{2}c_{rr}(R)
-
\frac{\Delta r^3}{6}c_{rrr}(R)
+
O(\Delta r^4).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-456}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-456}
\end{equation}

Substitute this expansion into the ghost construction:

\begin{equation}
c_g(R+\Delta r)
=
c(R)
-
\beta\Delta r\,c(R)
+
\frac{\Delta r^2}{2}c_{rr}(R)
-
\frac{\Delta r^3}{6}c_{rrr}(R)
+
O(\Delta r^4).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-457}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-457}
\end{equation}

The exact smooth continuation would be

\begin{equation}
c(R+\Delta r)
=
c(R)
+
\Delta r\,c_r(R)
+
\frac{\Delta r^2}{2}c_{rr}(R)
+
\frac{\Delta r^3}{6}c_{rrr}(R)
+
O(\Delta r^4).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-458}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-458}
\end{equation}

Apply \textbackslash\{\}(c\_r(R)=-\textbackslash\{\}beta c(R)\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
c(R+\Delta r)
=
c(R)
-
\beta\Delta r\,c(R)
+
\frac{\Delta r^2}{2}c_{rr}(R)
+
\frac{\Delta r^3}{6}c_{rrr}(R)
+
O(\Delta r^4).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-459}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-459}
\end{equation}

Subtract the exact continuation from the ghost continuation:

\begin{equation}
c_g(R+\Delta r)-c(R+\Delta r)
=
-\frac{\Delta r^3}{3}c_{rrr}(R)
+
O(\Delta r^4).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-460}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-460}
\end{equation}

Thus the ghost value error is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
c_g-c_{\mathrm{exact}}
=
O(\Delta r^3).
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-461}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-461}
\end{equation}

The ghost value enters the centred second derivative divided by \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}Delta r\textasciicircum{}2\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
c_{rr}^{\,g}(R)
=
\frac{
c(R-\Delta r)-2c(R)+c_g(R+\Delta r)
}{
\Delta r^2
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-462}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-462}
\end{equation}

Write the ghost value as

\begin{equation}
c_g(R+\Delta r)
=
c(R+\Delta r)
+
\varepsilon_g,
\tag{TRISO-DIS-463}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-463}
\end{equation}

where

\begin{equation}
\varepsilon_g
=
-\frac{\Delta r^3}{3}c_{rrr}(R)
+
O(\Delta r^4).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-464}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-464}
\end{equation}

Substitute:

\begin{equation}
c_{rr}^{\,g}(R)
=
\frac{
c(R-\Delta r)-2c(R)+c(R+\Delta r)
}{
\Delta r^2
}
+
\frac{\varepsilon_g}{\Delta r^2}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-465}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-465}
\end{equation}

The ordinary centred second derivative contributes

\begin{equation}
c_{rr}(R)+O(\Delta r^2).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-466}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-466}
\end{equation}

The ghost-error contribution is

\begin{equation}
\frac{\varepsilon_g}{\Delta r^2}
=
-\frac{\Delta r}{3}c_{rrr}(R)
+
O(\Delta r^2).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-467}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-467}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
c_{rr}^{\,g}(R)
=
c_{rr}(R)
-
\frac{\Delta r}{3}c_{rrr}(R)
+
O(\Delta r^2).
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-468}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-468}
\end{equation}

The first-derivative Robin closure remains second-order, but the second-derivative part is generically first-order at the boundary.

Hence the complete spherical surface operator has generic local spatial truncation

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathcal L_h[c](R)
=
\mathcal L[c](R)
+
O(\Delta r).
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-469}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-469}
\end{equation}

With forward Euler time stepping, the boundary local consistency is therefore generically

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
O(\Delta t)+O(\Delta r),
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-470}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-470}
\end{equation}

unless additional cancellation or superconvergence is demonstrated.

[CORRECTION] The previous claim of \textbackslash\{\}(O(\textbackslash\{\}Delta t)+O(\textbackslash\{\}Delta r\textasciicircum{}2)\textbackslash\{\}) for the complete Robin surface update was overstated. Only the centred first-derivative approximation was second-order.

[OPEN REVIEW FINDING R2-D01] The analytical overclaim is corrected, but the independent reviewer requires an actual grid-refinement study before this finding is closed. No global FTCS convergence order is claimed here.

\subsubsection*{16.10 Scope}

Equation (TRISO-DIS-443) belongs to the homogeneous Part-I FTCS benchmark.

For the physical five-layer problem, the same derivational structure may be applied to the OPyC layer by replacing \textbackslash\{\}(D\textbackslash\{\}) with \textbackslash\{\}(D\_5\textbackslash\{\}) and using the physically selected outer source and boundary parameters.

The formula must not be confused with the production WOS absorbing-boundary treatment.

No stability conclusion is drawn in this section.

\subsection*{17. FTCS monotonicity and stability analysis}

The original Ray notebook inferred a stability restriction from non-negative update coefficients.

That argument is useful, but the mathematical statement must be made precisely.

Non-negative coefficients with an appropriate row sum establish a monotonicity/maximum-principle style property for the homogeneous update. They are not automatically a necessary-and-sufficient characterization of every possible matrix stability notion.

This section first derives the coefficient restrictions and then states exactly what they prove.

\subsubsection*{17.1 Remove forcing for stability analysis}

The complete explicit update contains source terms.

To study propagation of perturbations, compare two numerical solutions subject to the same prescribed source.

Let their difference be

\begin{equation}
E_i^j
=
C_i^j-\widetilde C_i^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-500}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-500}
\end{equation}

Because both solutions have the same additive source, subtraction cancels that source.

Thus the error/perturbation equation is homogeneous.

Stability of the linear time-marching operator can therefore be analysed from the source-free amplification step

\begin{equation}
\mathbf E^{j+1}
=
\mathbf A\mathbf E^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-501}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-501}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection*{17.2 Interior-row coefficients}

For an interior homogeneous node, Section 13 gives

\begin{equation}
E_i^{j+1}
=
\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1-\frac1i
\right)
E_{i-1}^j
+
(1-2\mathrm{Fo})E_i^j
+
\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1+\frac1i
\right)
E_{i+1}^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-502}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-502}
\end{equation}

Define

\begin{equation}
a_i
=
\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1-\frac1i
\right),
\tag{TRISO-DIS-503}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-503}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
b_i
=
1-2\mathrm{Fo},
\tag{TRISO-DIS-504}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-504}
\end{equation}

and

\begin{equation}
d_i
=
\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1+\frac1i
\right).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-505}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-505}
\end{equation}

For \textbackslash\{\}(i\textbackslash\{\}ge1\textbackslash\{\}),

\begin{equation}
1-\frac1i\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-506}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-506}
\end{equation}

Since \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}mathrm\{Fo\}\textbackslash\{\}ge0\textbackslash\{\}),

\begin{equation}
a_i\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-507}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-507}
\end{equation}

Similarly,

\begin{equation}
1+\frac1i>0,
\tag{TRISO-DIS-508}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-508}
\end{equation}

so

\begin{equation}
d_i\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-509}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-509}
\end{equation}

The central coefficient is non-negative when

\begin{equation}
1-2\mathrm{Fo}\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-510}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-510}
\end{equation}

Rearrange:

\begin{equation}
2\mathrm{Fo}\le1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-511}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-511}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathrm{Fo}\le\frac12.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-512}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-512}
\end{equation}

Now add the three interior coefficients:

\begin{equation}
a_i+b_i+d_i
=
\mathrm{Fo}\left(1-\frac1i\right)
+
1-2\mathrm{Fo}
+
\mathrm{Fo}\left(1+\frac1i\right).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-513}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-513}
\end{equation}

Expand:

\begin{equation}
a_i+b_i+d_i
=
\mathrm{Fo}
-\frac{\mathrm{Fo}}i
+
1
-
2\mathrm{Fo}
+
\mathrm{Fo}
+
\frac{\mathrm{Fo}}i.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-514}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-514}
\end{equation}

Cancel the radial terms:

\begin{equation}
-\frac{\mathrm{Fo}}i
+
\frac{\mathrm{Fo}}i
=
0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-515}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-515}
\end{equation}

Cancel the diffusion contributions:

\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Fo}-2\mathrm{Fo}+\mathrm{Fo}=0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-516}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-516}
\end{equation}

Hence

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
a_i+b_i+d_i=1.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-517}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-517}
\end{equation}

When (TRISO-DIS-512) holds, an interior update is therefore a convex combination of the previous-time neighbouring values.

\subsubsection*{17.3 Centre-row coefficients}

Section 14 gives the homogeneous centre error update

\begin{equation}
E_0^{j+1}
=
(1-6\mathrm{Fo})E_0^j
+
6\mathrm{Fo}E_1^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-518}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-518}
\end{equation}

The neighbour coefficient satisfies

\begin{equation}
6\mathrm{Fo}\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-519}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-519}
\end{equation}

The centre coefficient is non-negative when

\begin{equation}
1-6\mathrm{Fo}\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-520}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-520}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
6\mathrm{Fo}\le1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-521}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-521}
\end{equation}

Hence

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathrm{Fo}\le\frac16.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-522}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-522}
\end{equation}

The centre-row sum is

\begin{equation}
(1-6\mathrm{Fo})+6\mathrm{Fo}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-523}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-523}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
(1-6\mathrm{Fo})+6\mathrm{Fo}=1.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-524}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-524}
\end{equation}

Under (TRISO-DIS-522), the centre row is also a convex combination.

\subsubsection*{17.4 Robin surface-row coefficients}

Section 16 gives the homogeneous surface error update

\begin{equation}
E_N^{j+1}
=
2\mathrm{Fo}E_{N-1}^j
+
\left[
1
-
2\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right)
\right]
E_N^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-525}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-525}
\end{equation}

The interior-neighbour coefficient is

\begin{equation}
2\mathrm{Fo}\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-526}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-526}
\end{equation}

The surface coefficient is non-negative when

\begin{equation}
1
-
2\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right)
\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-527}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-527}
\end{equation}

Move the second term to the other side:

\begin{equation}
1
\ge
2\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-528}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-528}
\end{equation}

For \textbackslash\{\}(h\textbackslash\{\}ge0\textbackslash\{\}), \textbackslash\{\}(D>0\textbackslash\{\}), and \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}Delta r>0\textbackslash\{\}),

\begin{equation}
\kappa\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-529}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-529}
\end{equation}

Therefore the denominator below is positive.

Divide:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathrm{Fo}
\le
\frac{
1
}{
2\left[
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right]
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-530}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-530}
\end{equation}

Now calculate the surface-row sum:

\begin{equation}
2\mathrm{Fo}
+
1
-
2\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-531}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-531}
\end{equation}

Expand the last term:

\begin{equation}
2\mathrm{Fo}
+
1
-
2\mathrm{Fo}
-
2\mathrm{Fo}\kappa
\left(
1+\frac1N
\right).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-532}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-532}
\end{equation}

Cancel \textbackslash\{\}(2\textbackslash\{\}mathrm\{Fo\}-2\textbackslash\{\}mathrm\{Fo\}\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\text{surface row sum}
=
1
-
2\mathrm{Fo}\kappa
\left(
1+\frac1N
\right).
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-533}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-533}
\end{equation}

For \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}kappa\textbackslash\{\}ge0\textbackslash\{\}),

\begin{equation}
\text{surface row sum}\le1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-534}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-534}
\end{equation}

Under the non-negativity restriction (TRISO-DIS-530),

\begin{equation}
\text{surface row sum}\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-535}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-535}
\end{equation}

Thus the Robin row is sub-convex: part of the previous concentration can leave through the external boundary.

\subsubsection*{17.5 Combined coefficient-non-negativity condition}

The three restrictions are

\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Fo}\le\frac12
\tag{TRISO-DIS-536}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-536}
\end{equation}

for ordinary interior nodes,

\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Fo}\le\frac16
\tag{TRISO-DIS-537}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-537}
\end{equation}

for the centre,

and

\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Fo}
\le
\frac{
1
}{
2\left[
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right]
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-538}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-538}
\end{equation}

for the Robin surface.

Because

\begin{equation}
\frac16<\frac12,
\tag{TRISO-DIS-539}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-539}
\end{equation}

the interior bound is never the controlling restriction once the centre node is included.

Therefore a sufficient coefficient-non-negativity condition for this homogeneous benchmark is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathrm{Fo}
\le
\min
\left\{
\frac16,
\,
\frac{
1
}{
2\left[
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right]
}
\right\}.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-540}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-540}
\end{equation}

This is the corrected form of the original notebook restriction.

\subsubsection*{17.6 Amplification matrix}

Collect the nodal errors into

\begin{equation}
\mathbf E^j
=
(E_0^j,E_1^j,\ldots,E_N^j)^T.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-541}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-541}
\end{equation}

The homogeneous update is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathbf E^{j+1}
=
\mathbf A\mathbf E^j.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-542}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-542}
\end{equation}

The matrix has size

\begin{equation}
\mathbf A\in\mathbb R^{(N+1)\times(N+1)}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-543}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-543}
\end{equation}

The centre row contains

\begin{equation}
A_{0,0}=1-6\mathrm{Fo},
\tag{TRISO-DIS-544}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-544}
\end{equation}

and

\begin{equation}
A_{0,1}=6\mathrm{Fo}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-545}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-545}
\end{equation}

For an ordinary interior row \textbackslash\{\}(i\textbackslash\{\}),

\begin{equation}
A_{i,i-1}
=
\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1-\frac1i
\right),
\tag{TRISO-DIS-546}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-546}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
A_{i,i}
=
1-2\mathrm{Fo},
\tag{TRISO-DIS-547}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-547}
\end{equation}

and

\begin{equation}
A_{i,i+1}
=
\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1+\frac1i
\right).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-548}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-548}
\end{equation}

The Robin surface row contains

\begin{equation}
A_{N,N-1}=2\mathrm{Fo},
\tag{TRISO-DIS-549}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-549}
\end{equation}

and

\begin{equation}
A_{N,N}
=
1
-
2\mathrm{Fo}
\left(
1+\kappa\left(1+\frac1N\right)
\right).
\tag{TRISO-DIS-550}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-550}
\end{equation}

All other entries are zero for this homogeneous tridiagonal benchmark.

\subsubsection*{17.7 \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}ell\_\textbackslash\{\}infty\textbackslash\{\}) stability under the monotonicity restriction}

The induced infinity norm of a matrix is

\begin{equation}
\|\mathbf A\|_\infty
=
\max_i
\sum_j
|A_{ij}|.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-551}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-551}
\end{equation}

Under (TRISO-DIS-540), every non-zero entry of \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}mathbf A\textbackslash\{\}) is non-negative.

Therefore

\begin{equation}
|A_{ij}|=A_{ij}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-552}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-552}
\end{equation}

For the centre row, the row sum is exactly

\begin{equation}
1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-553}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-553}
\end{equation}

For each ordinary interior row, the row sum is exactly

\begin{equation}
1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-554}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-554}
\end{equation}

For the Robin row, the row sum is at most

\begin{equation}
1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-555}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-555}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\|\mathbf A\|_\infty\le1.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-556}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-556}
\end{equation}

Apply the matrix norm inequality:

\begin{equation}
\|\mathbf E^{j+1}\|_\infty
=
\|\mathbf A\mathbf E^j\|_\infty
\le
\|\mathbf A\|_\infty
\|\mathbf E^j\|_\infty.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-557}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-557}
\end{equation}

Use (TRISO-DIS-556):

\begin{equation}
\|\mathbf E^{j+1}\|_\infty
\le
\|\mathbf E^j\|_\infty.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-558}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-558}
\end{equation}

Repeat the inequality over \textbackslash\{\}(j\textbackslash\{\}) steps:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\|\mathbf E^j\|_\infty
\le
\|\mathbf E^0\|_\infty.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-559}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-559}
\end{equation}

Thus the coefficient restriction (TRISO-DIS-540) is not merely a heuristic: for this assembled homogeneous benchmark it is a sufficient condition for non-amplification in the discrete \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}ell\_\textbackslash\{\}infty\textbackslash\{\}) norm.

\subsubsection*{17.8 Positivity and discrete maximum-principle interpretation}

Suppose

\begin{equation}
E_i^j\ge0
\tag{TRISO-DIS-560}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-560}
\end{equation}

for every node.

Under (TRISO-DIS-540), every amplification coefficient is non-negative.

Therefore every component of

\begin{equation}
\mathbf E^{j+1}
=
\mathbf A\mathbf E^j
\tag{TRISO-DIS-561}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-561}
\end{equation}

is also non-negative.

Hence the homogeneous update preserves non-negativity.

For an interior or centre row whose coefficients sum to one, the new value lies between the minimum and maximum of the contributing old values.

At the Robin boundary, the row sum is less than or equal to one because concentration can leave the domain.

This is the precise monotonicity/maximum-principle content of the notebook's coefficient argument.

\subsubsection*{17.9 Spectral-radius consequence}

For every square matrix,

\begin{equation}
\rho(\mathbf A)
\le
\|\mathbf A\|
\tag{TRISO-DIS-562}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-562}
\end{equation}

for any induced matrix norm.

Using the infinity norm,

\begin{equation}
\rho(\mathbf A)
\le
\|\mathbf A\|_\infty.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-563}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-563}
\end{equation}

Under (TRISO-DIS-540),

\begin{equation}
\|\mathbf A\|_\infty\le1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-564}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-564}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\rho(\mathbf A)\le1.
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-565}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-565}
\end{equation}

So the monotonicity restriction also provides a sufficient spectral-radius bound for this homogeneous assembled amplification matrix.

This does \textbf{not} prove that (TRISO-DIS-540) is necessary for spectral stability.

There may be parameter values with some negative coefficients for which

\begin{equation}
\rho(\mathbf A)\le1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-566}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-566}
\end{equation}

Determining the exact necessary-and-sufficient spectral stability region would require analysis of the eigenvalues of the specific amplification matrix.

\subsubsection*{17.10 Relation to the standard explicit-Euler eigenvalue condition}

Write a semi-discrete diffusion system abstractly as

\begin{equation}
\frac{d\mathbf C}{dt}
=
\mathbf L\mathbf C.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-567}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-567}
\end{equation}

Forward Euler gives

\begin{equation}
\mathbf C^{j+1}
=
\left(
\mathbf I+\Delta t\,\mathbf L
\right)
\mathbf C^j.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-568}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-568}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\mathbf A
=
\mathbf I+\Delta t\,\mathbf L.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-569}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-569}
\end{equation}

If \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}lambda\_\textbackslash\{\}ell\textbackslash\{\}) is an eigenvalue of \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}mathbf L\textbackslash\{\}), then the corresponding amplification eigenvalue is

\begin{equation}
g_\ell
=
1+\Delta t\,\lambda_\ell.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-570}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-570}
\end{equation}

For a real non-positive diffusion eigenvalue,

\begin{equation}
\lambda_\ell\le0,
\tag{TRISO-DIS-571}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-571}
\end{equation}

the scalar forward-Euler stability requirement is

\begin{equation}
|1+\Delta t\,\lambda_\ell|\le1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-572}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-572}
\end{equation}

For real \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}lambda\_\textbackslash\{\}ell\textbackslash\{\}le0\textbackslash\{\}), this is equivalent to

\begin{equation}
-1
\le
1+\Delta t\,\lambda_\ell
\le
1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-573}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-573}
\end{equation}

Subtract \textbackslash\{\}(1\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
-2
\le
\Delta t\,\lambda_\ell
\le
0.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-574}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-574}
\end{equation}

Because \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}lambda\_\textbackslash\{\}ell<0\textbackslash\{\}) for a decaying mode, the lower inequality gives

\begin{equation}
\Delta t
\le
\frac{2}{|\lambda_\ell|}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-575}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-575}
\end{equation}

For all modes,

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\Delta t
\le
\frac{2}{
\max_\ell|\lambda_\ell|
}
}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-576}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-576}
\end{equation}

would be the exact scalar forward-Euler restriction if the relevant semi-discrete operator has a real non-positive spectrum and is diagonalizable in the norm under consideration.

The present section does not compute \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}max|\textbackslash\{\}lambda\_\textbackslash\{\}ell|\textbackslash\{\}) for the spherical matrix.

Therefore (TRISO-DIS-576) is a framework for a sharper spectral analysis, not a completed numerical bound for this benchmark.

\subsubsection*{17.11 Important limitation: material interfaces}

The amplification matrix in Sections 17.6–17.10 corresponds to the homogeneous benchmark using:

\noindent\textbullet\ the centre row from Section 14;\\
\noindent\textbullet\ homogeneous interior rows from Section 13;\\
\noindent\textbullet\ the Robin surface row from Section 16.\\

The full five-layer problem contains discontinuous diffusivities and the interface transmission treatment from Section 15.

Its assembled operator is therefore different.

The bound

\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Fo}
\le
\min
\left\{
\frac16,
\frac1{
2[1+\kappa(1+1/N)]
}
\right\}
\tag{TRISO-DIS-577}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-577}
\end{equation}

must \textbf{not} be presented as a proved stability bound for an arbitrary five-layer discretisation.

A five-layer stability condition depends on the final chosen conservative spatial discretisation, the layer-specific diffusivities, mesh spacings, and interface treatment.

\subsubsection*{17.12 Correct status of the original notebook claim}

The original notebook's coefficient argument is therefore classified as follows.

[VERIFIED] Interior coefficient non-negativity requires

\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Fo}\le\frac12.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-578}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-578}
\end{equation}

[VERIFIED] Centre coefficient non-negativity requires

\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Fo}\le\frac16.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-579}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-579}
\end{equation}

[VERIFIED] Robin-surface coefficient non-negativity requires

\begin{equation}
\mathrm{Fo}
\le
\frac1{
2[1+\kappa(1+1/N)]
}.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-580}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-580}
\end{equation}

[VERIFIED] Their minimum is a sufficient monotonicity condition for the homogeneous benchmark.

[VERIFIED] Under that same condition, the assembled homogeneous amplification matrix satisfies

\begin{equation}
\|\mathbf A\|_\infty\le1
\tag{TRISO-DIS-581}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-581}
\end{equation}

and consequently

\begin{equation}
\rho(\mathbf A)\le1.
\tag{TRISO-DIS-582}\n\\label{eq:triso-dis-582}
\end{equation}

[NOT PROVED] The condition is necessary for spectral stability.

[NOT APPLICABLE WITHOUT RE-DERIVATION] The same bound is the exact stability condition for the final discontinuous-\textbackslash\{\}(D\textbackslash\{\}), five-layer discretisation.

\subsection*{18. Conservative finite-volume discretisation of the five-layer PDE}

The original FTCS derivation is a useful benchmark, but the actual five-layer PDE contains discontinuous material diffusivities.

A conservative finite-volume formulation is therefore derived directly from

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial c}{\partial t}
=
\frac1{r^2}
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}
\left(
r^2D\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}
\right)
+
S.
\tag{TRISO-FV-100}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-100}
\end{equation}

This choice is a mathematical discretisation of the verified continuum model. It does not replace or redefine the production WOS algorithm.

\subsubsection*{18.1 Spherical control-volume geometry}

Let cell \textbackslash\{\}(P\textbackslash\{\}) occupy

\begin{equation}
r_{P-\frac12}
<
r
<
r_{P+\frac12}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-101}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-101}
\end{equation}

Its west face is

\begin{equation}
r_w=r_{P-\frac12},
\tag{TRISO-FV-102}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-102}
\end{equation}

and its east face is

\begin{equation}
r_e=r_{P+\frac12}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-103}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-103}
\end{equation}

The corresponding spherical face areas are

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
A_w=4\pi r_w^2
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-104}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-104}
\end{equation}

and

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
A_e=4\pi r_e^2.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-105}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-105}
\end{equation}

The exact cell volume is

\begin{equation}
V_P
=
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}4\pi r^2\,dr.
\tag{TRISO-FV-106}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-106}
\end{equation}

Evaluate the integral:

\begin{equation}
V_P
=
4\pi
\left[
\frac{r^3}{3}
\right]_{r_w}^{r_e}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-107}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-107}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
V_P
=
\frac{4\pi}{3}
\left(
r_e^3-r_w^3
\right).
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-108}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-108}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection*{18.1.1 Frozen finite-volume unknown and representative coordinate}

The canonical finite-volume unknown remains the \textbf{exact spherical cell average} defined later in (TRISO-FV-118). It is not redefined as a point value.

For geometry and two-point flux reconstruction, assign each cell a representative radial coordinate equal to its spherical volume centroid:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
r_P
=
\frac{
\displaystyle
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
r\,4\pi r^2\,dr
}{
\displaystyle
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
4\pi r^2\,dr
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-109A}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-109a}
\end{equation}

Evaluate the numerator:

\begin{equation}
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
4\pi r^3\,dr
=
\pi
\left(
r_e^4-r_w^4
\right).
\tag{TRISO-FV-109B}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-109b}
\end{equation}

Use the exact volume (TRISO-FV-108):

\begin{equation}
V_P
=
\frac{4\pi}{3}
\left(
r_e^3-r_w^3
\right).
\tag{TRISO-FV-109C}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-109c}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
r_P
=
\frac34
\frac{
r_e^4-r_w^4
}{
r_e^3-r_w^3
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-109D}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-109d}
\end{equation}

The canonical representation is therefore:

\noindent\textbullet\ \textbackslash\{\}(C\_P\textbackslash\{\}): exact spherical volume average over cell \textbackslash\{\}(P\textbackslash\{\});\\
\noindent\textbullet\ \textbackslash\{\}(r\_P\textbackslash\{\}): spherical volume-centroid coordinate used as the representative location of that average in two-point reconstruction;\\
\noindent\textbullet\ \textbackslash\{\}(r\_\{P+1/2\}\textbackslash\{\}): physical face coordinate;\\
\noindent\textbullet\ \textbackslash\{\}(r\_\{P+1\}-r\_P\textbackslash\{\}): distance between representative cell coordinates;\\
\noindent\textbullet\ \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}delta r\_P=r\_\{P+1/2\}-r\_P\textbackslash\{\}): distance from cell \textbackslash\{\}(P\textbackslash\{\})'s representative coordinate to its east face;\\
\noindent\textbullet\ \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}delta r\_\{P+1\}=r\_\{P+1\}-r\_\{P+1/2\}\textbackslash\{\}): distance from the shared face to the neighbouring representative coordinate.\\

At a material interface, the physical interface is aligned with a face \textbackslash\{\}(r\_\{P+1/2\}\textbackslash\{\}).

At the outer boundary,

\begin{equation}
\delta r_R=R-r_{M-1}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-109E}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-109e}
\end{equation}

[IMPORTANT] The face-gradient formulas treat the exact cell averages as reconstructed values located at their volume centroids. That reconstruction is an approximation whose spatial order must be established in the subsequent accuracy study; it is not part of the exact control-volume balance.

\subsubsection*{18.2 Integrate conservation over one spherical cell}

Multiply (TRISO-FV-100) by the spherical volume element

\begin{equation}
4\pi r^2\,dr.
\tag{TRISO-FV-109}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-109}
\end{equation}

This gives

\begin{equation}
4\pi r^2
\frac{\partial c}{\partial t}\,dr
=
4\pi
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}
\left(
r^2D\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}
\right)dr
+
4\pi r^2S\,dr.
\tag{TRISO-FV-110}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-110}
\end{equation}

Integrate from \textbackslash\{\}(r\_w\textbackslash\{\}) to \textbackslash\{\}(r\_e\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
4\pi r^2
\frac{\partial c}{\partial t}\,dr
=
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
4\pi
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}
\left(
r^2D\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}
\right)dr
+
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
4\pi r^2S\,dr.
\tag{TRISO-FV-111}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-111}
\end{equation}

Evaluate the derivative integral:

\begin{equation}
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
4\pi
\frac{\partial}{\partial r}
\left(
r^2D\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}
\right)dr
=
4\pi
\left[
r^2D\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}
\right]_{r_w}^{r_e}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-112}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-112}
\end{equation}

Expand the boundary evaluation:

\begin{equation}
4\pi
\left[
r^2D\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}
\right]_{r_w}^{r_e}
=
A_eD_e
\left.\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}\right|_e
-
A_wD_w
\left.\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}\right|_w.
\tag{TRISO-FV-113}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-113}
\end{equation}

Define the outward radial Fickian flux

\begin{equation}
J_r=-D\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-114}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-114}
\end{equation}

Then

\begin{equation}
D\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}=-J_r.
\tag{TRISO-FV-115}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-115}
\end{equation}

Substitute into (TRISO-FV-113):

\begin{equation}
A_eD_e c_r|_e-A_wD_wc_r|_w
=
-A_eJ_e+A_wJ_w.
\tag{TRISO-FV-116}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-116}
\end{equation}

Thus the integrated conservation equation is

\begin{equation}
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
4\pi r^2
\frac{\partial c}{\partial t}\,dr
=
A_wJ_w-A_eJ_e
+
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}4\pi r^2S\,dr.
\tag{TRISO-FV-117}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-117}
\end{equation}

This is the discrete starting point: accumulation equals inward face flow minus outward face flow plus generation.

\subsubsection*{18.3 Cell-average unknown}

Define the volume-averaged concentration

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
C_P(t)
=
\frac1{V_P}
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
c(r,t)\,4\pi r^2\,dr.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-118}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-118}
\end{equation}

Multiply by \textbackslash\{\}(V\_P\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
V_PC_P
=
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
c\,4\pi r^2\,dr.
\tag{TRISO-FV-119}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-119}
\end{equation}

For a fixed mesh, \textbackslash\{\}(V\_P\textbackslash\{\}) is constant in time.

Differentiate:

\begin{equation}
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
4\pi r^2
\frac{\partial c}{\partial t}\,dr.
\tag{TRISO-FV-120}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-120}
\end{equation}

Define the volume-averaged source

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
S_P
=
\frac1{V_P}
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
S(r,t)\,4\pi r^2\,dr.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-121}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-121}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
S_PV_P
=
\int_{r_w}^{r_e}
S(r,t)\,4\pi r^2\,dr.
\tag{TRISO-FV-122}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-122}
\end{equation}

Substitute (TRISO-FV-120) and (TRISO-FV-122) into (TRISO-FV-117):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
A_wJ_w-A_eJ_e+S_PV_P.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-123}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-123}
\end{equation}

Equation (TRISO-FV-123) is an exact control-volume balance before face-flux approximation.

\subsubsection*{18.4 Interior face flux inside one material}

Let cells \textbackslash\{\}(P\textbackslash\{\}) and \textbackslash\{\}(E\textbackslash\{\}) share east face \textbackslash\{\}(e\textbackslash\{\}).

Assume the face lies inside one material with constant diffusivity \textbackslash\{\}(D\_e\textbackslash\{\}).

Let the cell-centre distance be

\begin{equation}
\delta r_{PE}=r_E-r_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-124}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-124}
\end{equation}

Approximate the face gradient by

\begin{equation}
\left.
\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}
\right|_e
\approx
\frac{C_E-C_P}{\delta r_{PE}}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-125}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-125}
\end{equation}

Fick's law gives

\begin{equation}
J_e
\approx
-D_e
\frac{C_E-C_P}{\delta r_{PE}}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-126}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-126}
\end{equation}

Reverse the numerator:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
J_e
\approx
D_e
\frac{C_P-C_E}{\delta r_{PE}}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-127}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-127}
\end{equation}

Similarly, for west neighbour \textbackslash\{\}(W\textbackslash\{\}),

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
J_w
\approx
D_w
\frac{C_W-C_P}{\delta r_{WP}}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-128}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-128}
\end{equation}

The sign convention is consistent with \textbackslash\{\}(J\_r>0\textbackslash\{\}) meaning outward radial transport.

\subsubsection*{18.5 Face crossing a material interface}

Now let face \textbackslash\{\}(e\textbackslash\{\}) coincide with an interface between two materials.

Let the distance from centre \textbackslash\{\}(P\textbackslash\{\}) to the interface be

\begin{equation}
\delta r_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-129}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-129}
\end{equation}

Let the distance from the interface to centre \textbackslash\{\}(E\textbackslash\{\}) be

\begin{equation}
\delta r_E.
\tag{TRISO-FV-130}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-130}
\end{equation}

Let the diffusivities be

\begin{equation}
D_P
\tag{TRISO-FV-131}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-131}
\end{equation}

and

\begin{equation}
D_E.
\tag{TRISO-FV-132}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-132}
\end{equation}

Let the ideal interface concentration be \textbackslash\{\}(C\_I\textbackslash\{\}).

The flux from \textbackslash\{\}(P\textbackslash\{\}) to the interface is

\begin{equation}
J_e
=
D_P
\frac{C_P-C_I}{\delta r_P}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-133}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-133}
\end{equation}

The flux from the interface to \textbackslash\{\}(E\textbackslash\{\}) is

\begin{equation}
J_e
=
D_E
\frac{C_I-C_E}{\delta r_E}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-134}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-134}
\end{equation}

Solve the first equation for the concentration drop:

\begin{equation}
C_P-C_I
=
J_e\frac{\delta r_P}{D_P}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-135}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-135}
\end{equation}

Solve the second equation for its drop:

\begin{equation}
C_I-C_E
=
J_e\frac{\delta r_E}{D_E}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-136}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-136}
\end{equation}

Add the two equations:

\begin{equation}
C_P-C_E
=
J_e
\left(
\frac{\delta r_P}{D_P}
+
\frac{\delta r_E}{D_E}
\right).
\tag{TRISO-FV-137}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-137}
\end{equation}

Solve for \textbackslash\{\}(J\_e\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
J_e
=
\frac{
C_P-C_E
}{
\dfrac{\delta r_P}{D_P}
+
\dfrac{\delta r_E}{D_E}
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-138}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-138}
\end{equation}

Define the face conductance per unit area

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
g_e
=
\left(
\frac{\delta r_P}{D_P}
+
\frac{\delta r_E}{D_E}
\right)^{-1}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-139}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-139}
\end{equation}

Then

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
J_e=g_e(C_P-C_E).
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-140}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-140}
\end{equation}

The interface law is therefore generated by adding diffusion resistances, exactly as in the continuous steady resistance derivation.

\subsubsection*{18.6 Effective face diffusivity}

Define the centre-to-centre distance

\begin{equation}
\delta r_{PE}
=
\delta r_P+\delta r_E.
\tag{TRISO-FV-141}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-141}
\end{equation}

Define \textbackslash\{\}(D\_e\textasciicircum{}\{\textbackslash\{\}mathrm\{eff\}\}\textbackslash\{\}) by

\begin{equation}
J_e
=
D_e^{\mathrm{eff}}
\frac{C_P-C_E}{\delta r_{PE}}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-142}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-142}
\end{equation}

Compare with (TRISO-FV-138):

\begin{equation}
\frac{D_e^{\mathrm{eff}}}{\delta r_{PE}}
=
\left(
\frac{\delta r_P}{D_P}
+
\frac{\delta r_E}{D_E}
\right)^{-1}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-143}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-143}
\end{equation}

Multiply by \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}delta r\_\{PE\}\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
D_e^{\mathrm{eff}}
=
\frac{
\delta r_P+\delta r_E
}{
\dfrac{\delta r_P}{D_P}
+
\dfrac{\delta r_E}{D_E}
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-144}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-144}
\end{equation}

For equal half-cell distances,

\begin{equation}
\delta r_P=\delta r_E,
\tag{TRISO-FV-145}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-145}
\end{equation}

this reduces to

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
D_e^{\mathrm{eff}}
=
\frac{2D_PD_E}{D_P+D_E}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-146}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-146}
\end{equation}

Thus the harmonic mean arises naturally from the conservative face-flux derivation.

\subsubsection*{18.7 Semi-discrete conservative equation}

Define the west conductance

\begin{equation}
G_w
=
\frac{A_wD_w^{\mathrm{eff}}}{\delta r_{WP}},
\tag{TRISO-FV-147}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-147}
\end{equation}

and the east conductance

\begin{equation}
G_e
=
\frac{A_eD_e^{\mathrm{eff}}}{\delta r_{PE}}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-148}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-148}
\end{equation}

Their units are

\begin{equation}
[G_w]=[G_e]=\mathrm{m^3\,s^{-1}}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-149}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-149}
\end{equation}

Using (TRISO-FV-127) and (TRISO-FV-128),

\begin{equation}
A_wJ_w
=
G_w(C_W-C_P),
\tag{TRISO-FV-150}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-150}
\end{equation}

and

\begin{equation}
A_eJ_e
=
G_e(C_P-C_E).
\tag{TRISO-FV-151}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-151}
\end{equation}

Substitute these into (TRISO-FV-123):

\begin{equation}
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
G_w(C_W-C_P)
-
G_e(C_P-C_E)
+
S_PV_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-152}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-152}
\end{equation}

Expand:

\begin{equation}
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
G_wC_W
-
G_wC_P
-
G_eC_P
+
G_eC_E
+
S_PV_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-153}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-153}
\end{equation}

Collect the central concentration:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
G_wC_W
-
(G_w+G_e)C_P
+
G_eC_E
+
S_PV_P.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-154}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-154}
\end{equation}

Divide by \textbackslash\{\}(V\_P\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
\frac{G_w}{V_P}C_W
-
\frac{G_w+G_e}{V_P}C_P
+
\frac{G_e}{V_P}C_E
+
S_P.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-155}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-155}
\end{equation}

This is the conservative semi-discrete equation for an ordinary spherical control volume.

\subsubsection*{18.8 Explicit Euler time discretisation}

Apply forward Euler:

\begin{equation}
\frac{
C_P^{j+1}-C_P^j
}{
\Delta t
}
=
\frac{G_w}{V_P}C_W^j
-
\frac{G_w+G_e}{V_P}C_P^j
+
\frac{G_e}{V_P}C_E^j
+
S_P^j.
\tag{TRISO-FV-156}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-156}
\end{equation}

Multiply by \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}Delta t\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
C_P^{j+1}-C_P^j
=
\frac{\Delta t\,G_w}{V_P}C_W^j
-
\frac{\Delta t(G_w+G_e)}{V_P}C_P^j
+
\frac{\Delta t\,G_e}{V_P}C_E^j
+
S_P^j\Delta t.
\tag{TRISO-FV-157}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-157}
\end{equation}

Add \textbackslash\{\}(C\_P\textasciicircum{}j\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
C_P^{j+1}
=
\frac{\Delta t\,G_w}{V_P}C_W^j
+
\left[
1-
\frac{\Delta t(G_w+G_e)}{V_P}
\right]C_P^j
+
\frac{\Delta t\,G_e}{V_P}C_E^j
+
S_P^j\Delta t.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-158}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-158}
\end{equation}

This is an explicit conservative finite-volume update.

\subsubsection*{18.9 Exact discrete conservation over multiple cells}

Sum (TRISO-FV-123) over all control volumes \textbackslash\{\}(P=1,\textbackslash\{\}ldots,M\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\sum_{P=1}^{M}
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
\sum_{P=1}^{M}
(A_wJ_w-A_eJ_e)
+
\sum_{P=1}^{M}S_PV_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-159}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-159}
\end{equation}

At a shared internal face, the east flux of one cell is the west flux of the next cell.

For example,

\begin{equation}
-A_{e,P}J_{e,P}
+
A_{w,P+1}J_{w,P+1}
=
0
\tag{TRISO-FV-160}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-160}
\end{equation}

because

\begin{equation}
A_{e,P}=A_{w,P+1}
\tag{TRISO-FV-161}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-161}
\end{equation}

and the same single face flux is used:

\begin{equation}
J_{e,P}=J_{w,P+1}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-162}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-162}
\end{equation}

Therefore every internal-face contribution cancels pairwise.

Only the physical domain boundaries remain:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\frac{d}{dt}
\left(
\sum_PV_PC_P
\right)
=
\text{boundary inflow}
-
\text{boundary outflow}
+
\sum_PS_PV_P.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-163}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-163}
\end{equation}

This is the principal conservation advantage of the finite-volume construction.

\subsubsection*{18.10 Centre control volume}

For the central cell,

\begin{equation}
r_w=0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-164}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-164}
\end{equation}

Therefore its west-face area is

\begin{equation}
A_w=4\pi(0)^2.
\tag{TRISO-FV-165}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-165}
\end{equation}

Hence

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
A_w=0.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-166}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-166}
\end{equation}

The centre requires no artificial inward flux condition in the control-volume balance because the spherical face at \textbackslash\{\}(r=0\textbackslash\{\}) has zero area.

The central balance becomes

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
V_0\frac{dC_0}{dt}
=
-A_eJ_e
+
S_0V_0.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-167}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-167}
\end{equation}

Using the outward face-flux convention,

\begin{equation}
J_e
=
G_e^{(A=1)}(C_0-C_1),
\tag{TRISO-FV-168}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-168}
\end{equation}

where \textbackslash\{\}(G\_e\textasciicircum{}\{(A=1)\}\textbackslash\{\}) denotes the conductance per unit area.

Equivalently, using total conductance \textbackslash\{\}(G\_e\textbackslash\{\}),

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
V_0\frac{dC_0}{dt}
=
G_e(C_1-C_0)
+
S_0V_0.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-169}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-169}
\end{equation}

Thus centre regularity is built geometrically into the zero-area inner face.

\subsubsection*{18.11 Kernel-confined source}

For a cell entirely inside the kernel,

\begin{equation}
S_P=S_0
\tag{TRISO-FV-170}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-170}
\end{equation}

for the constant-source benchmark.

For a cell entirely outside the kernel,

\begin{equation}
S_P=0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-171}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-171}
\end{equation}

If a control-volume face is aligned with the kernel boundary \textbackslash\{\}(r\_1\textbackslash\{\}), no cell straddles the source discontinuity.

Then the discrete total generation is

\begin{equation}
\dot N_{\mathrm{gen}}^{\,h}
=
\sum_{P\in\mathrm{kernel}}
S_0V_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-172}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-172}
\end{equation}

Because the kernel control volumes exactly partition \textbackslash\{\}(0<r<r\_1\textbackslash\{\}),

\begin{equation}
\sum_{P\in\mathrm{kernel}}V_P
=
\frac{4\pi r_1^3}{3}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-173}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-173}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\dot N_{\mathrm{gen}}^{\,h}
=
\frac{4\pi S_0r_1^3}{3}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-174}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-174}
\end{equation}

The aligned finite-volume source inventory exactly reproduces the continuous total generation for constant \textbackslash\{\}(S\_0\textbackslash\{\}).

\subsubsection*{18.12 Cell-centred Robin boundary closure}

At the physical outer surface,

\begin{equation}
r=R.
\tag{TRISO-FV-175}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-175}
\end{equation}

Let the centre of the outermost OPyC control volume be at

\begin{equation}
r_P<R.
\tag{TRISO-FV-176}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-176}
\end{equation}

Define the centre-to-surface distance

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\delta r_R=R-r_P.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-177}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-177}
\end{equation}

Let the outer OPyC diffusivity be

\begin{equation}
D_5.
\tag{TRISO-FV-178}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-178}
\end{equation}

Let the physical surface concentration be

\begin{equation}
C_R.
\tag{TRISO-FV-179}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-179}
\end{equation}

The outer-cell unknown is the cell-centred or cell-average concentration

\begin{equation}
C_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-180}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-180}
\end{equation}

These two concentrations are not silently identified.

\#\#\#\# 18.12.1 Half-cell diffusion relation

Approximate the OPyC concentration gradient between the outer cell centre and the physical surface by

\begin{equation}
\left.
\frac{\partial c}{\partial r}
\right|_{P\rightarrow R}
\approx
\frac{C_R-C_P}{\delta r_R}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-181}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-181}
\end{equation}

Fick's law gives the outward radial flux

\begin{equation}
J_R
=
-D_5
\frac{C_R-C_P}{\delta r_R}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-182}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-182}
\end{equation}

Reverse the concentration difference:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
J_R
=
D_5
\frac{C_P-C_R}{\delta r_R}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-183}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-183}
\end{equation}

Solve for the half-cell concentration drop:

\begin{equation}
C_P-C_R
=
J_R\frac{\delta r_R}{D_5}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-184}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-184}
\end{equation}

The quantity

\begin{equation}
\frac{\delta r_R}{D_5}
\tag{TRISO-FV-185}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-185}
\end{equation}

is the diffusion resistance per unit area of the outer half-cell.

\#\#\#\# 18.12.2 External film relation

The physical Robin law is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
J_R
=
h(C_R-c_\infty).
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-186}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-186}
\end{equation}

Solve for the film concentration drop:

\begin{equation}
C_R-c_\infty
=
\frac{J_R}{h}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-187}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-187}
\end{equation}

The external-film resistance per unit area is therefore

\begin{equation}
\frac1h.
\tag{TRISO-FV-188}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-188}
\end{equation}

\#\#\#\# 18.12.3 Add the two series concentration drops

Write the total cell-centre-to-bulk concentration difference as

\begin{equation}
C_P-c_\infty
=
(C_P-C_R)
+
(C_R-c_\infty).
\tag{TRISO-FV-189}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-189}
\end{equation}

Substitute the half-cell drop (TRISO-FV-184):

\begin{equation}
C_P-c_\infty
=
J_R\frac{\delta r_R}{D_5}
+
(C_R-c_\infty).
\tag{TRISO-FV-190}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-190}
\end{equation}

Substitute the film drop (TRISO-FV-187):

\begin{equation}
C_P-c_\infty
=
J_R\frac{\delta r_R}{D_5}
+
\frac{J_R}{h}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-191}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-191}
\end{equation}

Factor out \textbackslash\{\}(J\_R\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
C_P-c_\infty
=
J_R
\left(
\frac{\delta r_R}{D_5}
+
\frac1h
\right).
\tag{TRISO-FV-192}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-192}
\end{equation}

Solve for the outward boundary flux:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
J_R
=
\frac{
C_P-c_\infty
}{
\dfrac{\delta r_R}{D_5}
+
\dfrac1h
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-193}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-193}
\end{equation}

Thus the half-cell diffusion resistance and external-film resistance add in series.

\#\#\#\# 18.12.4 Effective boundary transfer coefficient

Define the effective cell-centre-to-bulk transfer coefficient

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
h_{\mathrm{eff}}
=
\left(
\frac{\delta r_R}{D_5}
+
\frac1h
\right)^{-1}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-194}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-194}
\end{equation}

Then

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
J_R
=
h_{\mathrm{eff}}
(C_P-c_\infty).
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-195}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-195}
\end{equation}

Multiply numerator and denominator of (TRISO-FV-194) by \textbackslash\{\}(hD\_5\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
h_{\mathrm{eff}}
=
\frac{hD_5}{
h\delta r_R+D_5
}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-196}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-196}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
h_{\mathrm{eff}}
=
\frac{hD_5}{
D_5+h\delta r_R
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-197}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-197}
\end{equation}

The units are

\begin{equation}
[h_{\mathrm{eff}}]
=
\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-198}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-198}
\end{equation}

\#\#\#\# 18.12.5 Recover the physical surface concentration

The surface concentration can also be obtained explicitly.

From (TRISO-FV-187),

\begin{equation}
C_R
=
c_\infty+\frac{J_R}{h}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-199}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-199}
\end{equation}

Substitute (TRISO-FV-193):

\begin{equation}
C_R
=
c_\infty
+
\frac1h
\frac{
C_P-c_\infty
}{
\dfrac{\delta r_R}{D_5}
+
\dfrac1h
}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-200}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-200}
\end{equation}

Multiply the second term's denominator by \textbackslash\{\}(h\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
C_R
=
c_\infty
+
\frac{
C_P-c_\infty
}{
1+\dfrac{h\delta r_R}{D_5}
}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-201}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-201}
\end{equation}

Define the half-cell boundary Biot number

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathrm{Bi}_R
=
\frac{h\delta r_R}{D_5}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-202}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-202}
\end{equation}

Then

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
C_R
=
c_\infty
+
\frac{
C_P-c_\infty
}{
1+\mathrm{Bi}_R
}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-203}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-203}
\end{equation}

This expression keeps the cell-centre and physical surface concentrations distinct.

\#\#\#\# 18.12.6 Limiting checks

If

\begin{equation}
h\to0,
\tag{TRISO-FV-204}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-204}
\end{equation}

then

\begin{equation}
\frac1h\to\infty.
\tag{TRISO-FV-205}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-205}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
h_{\mathrm{eff}}\to0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-206}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-206}
\end{equation}

Hence

\begin{equation}
J_R\to0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-207}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-207}
\end{equation}

This recovers the insulating Neumann limit.

If

\begin{equation}
\delta r_R\to0,
\tag{TRISO-FV-208}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-208}
\end{equation}

then the half-cell resistance vanishes:

\begin{equation}
\frac{\delta r_R}{D_5}\to0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-209}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-209}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
h_{\mathrm{eff}}\to h.
\tag{TRISO-FV-210}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-210}
\end{equation}

Thus the cell-centred closure approaches the physical Robin law as the outer cell centre approaches the surface.

If

\begin{equation}
h\to\infty,
\tag{TRISO-FV-211}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-211}
\end{equation}

then the film resistance vanishes:

\begin{equation}
\frac1h\to0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-212}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-212}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
h_{\mathrm{eff}}
\to
\frac{D_5}{\delta r_R}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-213}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-213}
\end{equation}

The resulting flux is

\begin{equation}
J_R
\to
\frac{D_5}{\delta r_R}
(C_P-c_\infty).
\tag{TRISO-FV-214}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-214}
\end{equation}

This is the expected half-cell diffusion flux to a prescribed Dirichlet surface concentration \textbackslash\{\}(C\_R=c\_\textbackslash\{\}infty\textbackslash\{\}).

Unlike the ghost-point formula, this cell-centred resistance closure remains finite in the \textbackslash\{\}(h\textbackslash\{\}to\textbackslash\{\}infty\textbackslash\{\}) limit.

\#\#\#\# 18.12.7 Outer-face amount conductance

The physical outer area is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
A_R=4\pi R^2.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-215}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-215}
\end{equation}

Define the total outer-boundary conductance

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
G_R=A_Rh_{\mathrm{eff}}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-216}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-216}
\end{equation}

Its units are

\begin{equation}
[G_R]
=
\mathrm{m^2}
\times
\mathrm{m\,s^{-1}}
=
\mathrm{m^3\,s^{-1}}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-217}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-217}
\end{equation}

The outward amount rate is

\begin{equation}
A_RJ_R
=
G_R(C_P-c_\infty).
\tag{TRISO-FV-218}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-218}
\end{equation}

\#\#\#\# 18.12.8 Final outer-cell semi-discrete balance

For the outermost control volume, the exact balance is

\begin{equation}
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
A_wJ_w
-
A_RJ_R
+
S_PV_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-219}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-219}
\end{equation}

The west-face amount rate is

\begin{equation}
A_wJ_w
=
G_w(C_W-C_P).
\tag{TRISO-FV-220}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-220}
\end{equation}

The outer amount rate is

\begin{equation}
A_RJ_R
=
G_R(C_P-c_\infty).
\tag{TRISO-FV-221}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-221}
\end{equation}

Substitute both:

\begin{equation}
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
G_w(C_W-C_P)
-
G_R(C_P-c_\infty)
+
S_PV_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-222}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-222}
\end{equation}

Expand the west term:

\begin{equation}
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
G_wC_W
-
G_wC_P
-
G_R(C_P-c_\infty)
+
S_PV_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-223}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-223}
\end{equation}

Expand the boundary term:

\begin{equation}
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
G_wC_W
-
G_wC_P
-
G_RC_P
+
G_Rc_\infty
+
S_PV_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-224}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-224}
\end{equation}

Collect the cell-centre concentration:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
G_wC_W
-
(G_w+G_R)C_P
+
G_Rc_\infty
+
S_PV_P.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-225}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-225}
\end{equation}

Divide by \textbackslash\{\}(V\_P\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
\frac{G_w}{V_P}C_W
-
\frac{G_w+G_R}{V_P}C_P
+
\frac{G_R}{V_P}c_\infty
+
S_P.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-226}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-226}
\end{equation}

For the benchmark

\begin{equation}
c_\infty=0,
\tag{TRISO-FV-227}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-227}
\end{equation}

this reduces to

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
\frac{G_w}{V_P}C_W
-
\frac{G_w+G_R}{V_P}C_P
+
S_P.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-228}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-228}
\end{equation}

For the physical five-layer kernel-confined source model, the outer OPyC cell has

\begin{equation}
S_P=0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-229}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-229}
\end{equation}

\#\#\#\# 18.12.9 Explicit Euler outer-cell update

Apply forward Euler to (TRISO-FV-226):

\begin{equation}
\frac{
C_P^{j+1}-C_P^j
}{
\Delta t
}
=
\frac{G_w}{V_P}C_W^j
-
\frac{G_w+G_R}{V_P}C_P^j
+
\frac{G_R}{V_P}c_\infty^j
+
S_P^j.
\tag{TRISO-FV-230}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-230}
\end{equation}

Multiply by \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}Delta t\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
C_P^{j+1}-C_P^j
=
\frac{\Delta tG_w}{V_P}C_W^j
-
\frac{\Delta t(G_w+G_R)}{V_P}C_P^j
+
\frac{\Delta tG_R}{V_P}c_\infty^j
+
S_P^j\Delta t.
\tag{TRISO-FV-231}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-231}
\end{equation}

Add \textbackslash\{\}(C\_P\textasciicircum{}j\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
C_P^{j+1}
=
\frac{\Delta tG_w}{V_P}C_W^j
+
\left[
1-\frac{\Delta t(G_w+G_R)}{V_P}
\right]C_P^j
+
\frac{\Delta tG_R}{V_P}c_\infty^j
+
S_P^j\Delta t.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-232}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-232}
\end{equation}

This closes the cell-centred Robin boundary without identifying the cell-centre concentration with the physical surface concentration.

\subsubsection*{18.13 Explicit-Euler positivity condition for an ordinary cell}

From (TRISO-FV-158), the neighbour coefficients are

\begin{equation}
\frac{\Delta t\,G_w}{V_P}\ge0
\tag{TRISO-FV-600}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-600}
\end{equation}

and

\begin{equation}
\frac{\Delta t\,G_e}{V_P}\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-601}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-601}
\end{equation}

The central coefficient is non-negative when

\begin{equation}
1-
\frac{\Delta t(G_w+G_e)}{V_P}
\ge0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-602}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-602}
\end{equation}

Rearrange:

\begin{equation}
\Delta t(G_w+G_e)
\le
V_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-603}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-603}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\Delta t
\le
\frac{V_P}{G_w+G_e}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-604}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-604}
\end{equation}

For every ordinary cell, a sufficient global coefficient-positivity restriction is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\Delta t
\le
\min_P
\frac{V_P}{G_w+G_e}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-605}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-605}
\end{equation}

This is the finite-volume analogue of the earlier homogeneous FTCS monotonicity restriction.

The outer Robin closure is now available in Section 18.12. The final global positivity bound must include its boundary conductance.

\subsubsection*{18.14 Global five-layer semi-discrete matrix}

Let the finite-volume mesh contain \textbackslash\{\}(M\textbackslash\{\}) spherical cells.

Index the cell-average concentrations by

\begin{equation}
\mathbf C(t)
=
(C_0,C_1,\ldots,C_{M-1})^T.
\tag{TRISO-FV-233}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-233}
\end{equation}

Define the diagonal volume matrix

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathbf V
=
\operatorname{diag}
(V_0,V_1,\ldots,V_{M-1}).
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-234}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-234}
\end{equation}

Every cell volume is positive:

\begin{equation}
V_P>0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-235}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-235}
\end{equation}

Let \textbackslash\{\}(G\_\{P+\textbackslash\{\}frac12\}\textbackslash\{\}) denote the total conductance of the face shared by cells \textbackslash\{\}(P\textbackslash\{\}) and \textbackslash\{\}(P+1\textbackslash\{\}).

At an ordinary same-material face,

\begin{equation}
G_{P+\frac12}
=
\frac{
A_{P+\frac12}D
}{
r_{P+1}-r_P
}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-236}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-236}
\end{equation}

At a material-interface face,

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
G_{P+\frac12}
=
A_{P+\frac12}
\left(
\frac{\delta r_P}{D_P}
+
\frac{\delta r_{P+1}}{D_{P+1}}
\right)^{-1}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-237}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-237}
\end{equation}

Thus the matrix assembly does not require a separate interface unknown.

\subsubsection*{18.15 Central-cell row}

The central-cell balance is

\begin{equation}
V_0\frac{dC_0}{dt}
=
G_{\frac12}(C_1-C_0)
+
S_0V_0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-238}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-238}
\end{equation}

Expand:

\begin{equation}
V_0\frac{dC_0}{dt}
=
-G_{\frac12}C_0
+
G_{\frac12}C_1
+
S_0V_0.
\tag{TRISO-FV-239}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-239}
\end{equation}

Therefore the first row of the transport matrix contains

\begin{equation}
K_{0,0}
=
-G_{\frac12},
\tag{TRISO-FV-240}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-240}
\end{equation}

and

\begin{equation}
K_{0,1}
=
G_{\frac12}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-241}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-241}
\end{equation}

\subsubsection*{18.16 Ordinary-cell row}

For cell \textbackslash\{\}(P\textbackslash\{\}), with

\begin{equation}
1\le P\le M-2,
\tag{TRISO-FV-242}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-242}
\end{equation}

the conservative balance is

\begin{equation}
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
G_{P-\frac12}(C_{P-1}-C_P)
+
G_{P+\frac12}(C_{P+1}-C_P)
+
S_PV_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-243}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-243}
\end{equation}

Expand the west-face term:

\begin{equation}
G_{P-\frac12}(C_{P-1}-C_P)
=
G_{P-\frac12}C_{P-1}
-
G_{P-\frac12}C_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-244}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-244}
\end{equation}

Expand the east-face term:

\begin{equation}
G_{P+\frac12}(C_{P+1}-C_P)
=
G_{P+\frac12}C_{P+1}
-
G_{P+\frac12}C_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-245}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-245}
\end{equation}

Substitute:

\begin{equation}
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
G_{P-\frac12}C_{P-1}
-
G_{P-\frac12}C_P
+
G_{P+\frac12}C_{P+1}
-
G_{P+\frac12}C_P
+
S_PV_P.
\tag{TRISO-FV-246}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-246}
\end{equation}

Collect the central coefficient:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
V_P\frac{dC_P}{dt}
=
G_{P-\frac12}C_{P-1}
-
\left(
G_{P-\frac12}+G_{P+\frac12}
\right)C_P
+
G_{P+\frac12}C_{P+1}
+
S_PV_P.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-247}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-247}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
K_{P,P-1}
=
G_{P-\frac12},
\tag{TRISO-FV-248}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-248}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
K_{P,P}
=
-\left(
G_{P-\frac12}+G_{P+\frac12}
\right),
\tag{TRISO-FV-249}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-249}
\end{equation}

and

\begin{equation}
K_{P,P+1}
=
G_{P+\frac12}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-250}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-250}
\end{equation}

These formulas remain valid when either face is a material interface because the corresponding \textbackslash\{\}(G\textbackslash\{\}) already contains the resistance-weighted discontinuous-\textbackslash\{\}(D\textbackslash\{\}) coupling.

\subsubsection*{18.17 Outermost-cell row}

Let the outermost cell index be

\begin{equation}
P=M-1.
\tag{TRISO-FV-251}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-251}
\end{equation}

Section 18.12 gives

\begin{equation}
V_{M-1}\frac{dC_{M-1}}{dt}
=
G_{M-\frac32}C_{M-2}
-
\left(
G_{M-\frac32}+G_R
\right)C_{M-1}
+
G_Rc_\infty
+
S_{M-1}V_{M-1}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-252}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-252}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
K_{M-1,M-2}
=
G_{M-\frac32},
\tag{TRISO-FV-253}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-253}
\end{equation}

and

\begin{equation}
K_{M-1,M-1}
=
-\left(
G_{M-\frac32}+G_R
\right).
\tag{TRISO-FV-254}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-254}
\end{equation}

The external concentration enters as a forcing term rather than as another particle unknown.

\subsubsection*{18.18 Assemble the transport matrix}

Define the source vector

\begin{equation}
\mathbf S
=
(S_0,S_1,\ldots,S_{M-1})^T.
\tag{TRISO-FV-255}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-255}
\end{equation}

Define the external-boundary forcing vector

\begin{equation}
\mathbf b_\infty
=
(0,0,\ldots,0,G_Rc_\infty)^T.
\tag{TRISO-FV-256}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-256}
\end{equation}

The complete semi-discrete system is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathbf V
\frac{d\mathbf C}{dt}
=
\mathbf K\mathbf C
+
\mathbf V\mathbf S
+
\mathbf b_\infty.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-257}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-257}
\end{equation}

The matrix \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}mathbf K\textbackslash\{\}) is tridiagonal:

\begin{equation}
\mathbf K
=
\begin{pmatrix}
-G_{\frac12}
&
G_{\frac12}
&
0
&
\cdots
&
0
\\
G_{\frac12}
&
-(G_{\frac12}+G_{\frac32})
&
G_{\frac32}
&
\ddots
&
\vdots
\\
0
&
G_{\frac32}
&
-(G_{\frac32}+G_{\frac52})
&
\ddots
&
0
\\
\vdots
&
\ddots
&
\ddots
&
\ddots
&
G_{M-\frac32}
\\
0
&
\cdots
&
0
&
G_{M-\frac32}
&
-(G_{M-\frac32}+G_R)
\end{pmatrix}.
\tag{TRISO-FV-258}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-258}
\end{equation}

Because each shared-face conductance appears identically in the two neighbouring rows,

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathbf K=\mathbf K^T.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-259}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-259}
\end{equation}

The time-evolution operator in concentration coordinates is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathbf L
=
\mathbf V^{-1}\mathbf K.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-260}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-260}
\end{equation}

The matrix \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}mathbf L\textbackslash\{\}) is generally not symmetric because cell volumes differ with radius.

\subsubsection*{18.19 Weighted self-adjoint structure of the semi-discrete operator}

Define the discrete volume-weighted inner product

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\langle\mathbf x,\mathbf y\rangle_V
=
\mathbf x^T\mathbf V\mathbf y.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-261}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-261}
\end{equation}

Evaluate

\begin{equation}
\langle\mathbf x,\mathbf L\mathbf y\rangle_V
=
\mathbf x^T\mathbf V\mathbf L\mathbf y.
\tag{TRISO-FV-262}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-262}
\end{equation}

Use \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}mathbf L=\textbackslash\{\}mathbf V\textasciicircum{}\{-1\}\textbackslash\{\}mathbf K\textbackslash\{\}):

\begin{equation}
\mathbf V\mathbf L
=
\mathbf K.
\tag{TRISO-FV-263}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-263}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\langle\mathbf x,\mathbf L\mathbf y\rangle_V
=
\mathbf x^T\mathbf K\mathbf y.
\tag{TRISO-FV-264}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-264}
\end{equation}

Because \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}mathbf K=\textbackslash\{\}mathbf K\textasciicircum{}T\textbackslash\{\}),

\begin{equation}
\mathbf x^T\mathbf K\mathbf y
=
\mathbf y^T\mathbf K\mathbf x.
\tag{TRISO-FV-265}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-265}
\end{equation}

Reverse the preceding steps:

\begin{equation}
\mathbf y^T\mathbf K\mathbf x
=
\langle\mathbf L\mathbf x,\mathbf y\rangle_V.
\tag{TRISO-FV-266}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-266}
\end{equation}

Hence

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\langle\mathbf x,\mathbf L\mathbf y\rangle_V
=
\langle\mathbf L\mathbf x,\mathbf y\rangle_V.
}
\tag{TRISO-FV-267}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-267}
\end{equation}

Thus the conservative semi-discrete diffusion operator is self-adjoint in the volume-weighted discrete inner product.

This is the discrete analogue of the continuum \textbackslash\{\}(r\textasciicircum{}2\textbackslash\{\})-weighted self-adjoint structure.

\subsubsection*{18.20 Negative-semidefinite diffusion form}

For any vector \textbackslash\{\}(\textbackslash\{\}mathbf x\textbackslash\{\}),

\begin{equation}
\mathbf x^T\mathbf K\mathbf x
\tag{TRISO-FV-268}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-268}
\end{equation}

can be grouped face-by-face.

An internal face between \textbackslash\{\}(P\textbackslash\{\}) and \textbackslash\{\}(P+1\textbackslash\{\}) contributes

\begin{equation}
-G_{P+\frac12}x_P^2
+
2G_{P+\frac12}x_Px_{P+1}
-
G_{P+\frac12}x_{P+1}^2.
\tag{TRISO-FV-269}\n\\label{eq:triso-fv-269}
\end{equation}
