\section*{21. Method 2 stochastic diffusion and first-passage formulation}

\subsection*{21.1 Diffusion process and backward generator}

[EXACT] In one homogeneous material with constant diffusivity (D),

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial c}{\partial t}=D\nabla^2c.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-001}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-001}
\end{equation}

[DEFINITION] Let (X\_t) be the diffusion process generated by the same operator.

Its infinitesimal generator is

\begin{equation}
\mathcal L f=D\nabla^2f.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-002}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-002}
\end{equation}

[DEFINITION] Let (T) be the first exit time from a prescribed region.

Define

\begin{equation}
u(\mathbf x,s)
=
\mathbb E_{\mathbf x}\left[e^{-sT}g(X_T)\right].
\tag{TRISO-FPT-003}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-003}
\end{equation}

The backward first-exit equation is

\begin{equation}
\mathcal Lu=su.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-004}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-004}
\end{equation}

Substitute TRISO-FPT-002:

\begin{equation}
D\nabla^2u=su.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-005}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-005}
\end{equation}

[ASSUMPTION] Under spherical symmetry, (u=u(r,s)).

The radial Laplacian is

\begin{equation}
\nabla^2u
=
\frac{1}{r^2}\frac{d}{dr}\left(r^2\frac{du}{dr}\right).
\tag{TRISO-FPT-006}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-006}
\end{equation}

Substitute into TRISO-FPT-005:

\begin{equation}
D\frac{1}{r^2}\frac{d}{dr}(r^2u')=su.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-007}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-007}
\end{equation}

Apply the product rule:

\begin{equation}
\frac{d}{dr}(r^2u')=2ru'+r^2u''.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-008}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-008}
\end{equation}

Divide by (r\textasciicircum{}2):

\begin{equation}
\frac{1}{r^2}\frac{d}{dr}(r^2u')
=
u''+\frac{2}{r}u'.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-009}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-009}
\end{equation}

Hence

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
D\left(u''+\frac{2}{r}u'\right)=su.
}
\tag{TRISO-FPT-010}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-010}
\end{equation}

This is the backward equation associated with the same diffusion operator as the forward continuum model.

\subsection*{21.2 Uniform-volume initial position}

[ASSUMPTION] Production histories are initially uniform in kernel volume.

Let (U\textbackslash\{\}sim\textbackslash\{\}mathcal U(0,1)).

The enclosed-volume fraction at radius (r) is

\begin{equation}
U
=
\frac{(4\pi/3)r^3}{(4\pi/3)R_1^3}.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-001}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-001}
\end{equation}

Cancel (4\textbackslash\{\}pi/3):

\begin{equation}
U=\frac{r^3}{R_1^3}.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-002}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-002}
\end{equation}

Multiply by (R\_1\textasciicircum{}3):

\begin{equation}
r^3=R_1^3U.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-003}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-003}
\end{equation}

Take the real cube root:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{r=R_1U^{1/3}.}
\tag{TRISO-WOS-004}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-004}
\end{equation}

\subsection*{21.3 Production interface law}

[INFERRED FROM CODE] For the frozen (K=1) interface rule,

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
p_{i\rightarrow j}
=
\frac{D_j}{D_i+D_j}.
}
\tag{TRISO-WOS-005}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-005}
\end{equation}

The reflection probability is

\begin{equation}
p_{i\rightarrow i}
=
1-p_{i\rightarrow j}.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-006}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-006}
\end{equation}

Substitute TRISO-WOS-005:

\begin{equation}
p_{i\rightarrow i}
=
1-\frac{D_j}{D_i+D_j}.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-007}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-007}
\end{equation}

Write the unit term over the same denominator:

\begin{equation}
p_{i\rightarrow i}
=
\frac{D_i+D_j}{D_i+D_j}
-
\frac{D_j}{D_i+D_j}.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-008}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-008}
\end{equation}

Subtract the numerators:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
p_{i\rightarrow i}
=
\frac{D_i}{D_i+D_j}.
}
\tag{TRISO-WOS-009}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-009}
\end{equation}

[DEFINITION] The finite capture distance is (epsilon).

[DEFINITION] The reinsertion distance is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{\delta=\alpha\epsilon.}
\tag{TRISO-WOS-010}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-010}
\end{equation}

[INFERRED FROM CODE] The interface decision contributes zero physical time.

[IMPORTANT] These finite-capture semantics define Process A and remain distinct from the exact-interface process below.

\section*{22. Exact spherical-shell first-passage theory}

\subsection*{22.1 Outer-exit joint transform}

[DEFINITION] Consider a homogeneous shell

\begin{equation}
a<r<b.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-020}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-020}
\end{equation}

Define the first exit time (T) and the outer-exit joint transform

\begin{equation}
G_b(r,s)
=
\mathbb E_r\left[e^{-sT}\mathbf 1_{\{R_T=b\}}\right].
\tag{TRISO-FPT-021}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-021}
\end{equation}

TRISO-FPT-010 gives

\begin{equation}
D\left(G_b''+\frac{2}{r}G_b'\right)=sG_b.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-022}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-022}
\end{equation}

The outer-exit boundary conditions are

\begin{equation}
G_b(a,s)=0,
\tag{TRISO-FPT-023}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-023}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
G_b(b,s)=1.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-024}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-024}
\end{equation}

Introduce

\begin{equation}
v(r)=rG_b(r,s).
\tag{TRISO-FPT-025}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-025}
\end{equation}

Then

\begin{equation}
G_b=\frac{v}{r}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-026}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-026}
\end{equation}

Differentiate:

\begin{equation}
G_b'
=
\frac{v'}{r}
-
\frac{v}{r^2}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-027}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-027}
\end{equation}

Differentiate again:

\begin{equation}
G_b''
=
\frac{v''}{r}
-
\frac{2v'}{r^2}
+
\frac{2v}{r^3}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-028}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-028}
\end{equation}

Also,

\begin{equation}
\frac{2}{r}G_b'
=
\frac{2v'}{r^2}
-
\frac{2v}{r^3}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-029}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-029}
\end{equation}

Add TRISO-FPT-028 and TRISO-FPT-029:

\begin{equation}
G_b''+\frac{2}{r}G_b'
=
\frac{v''}{r}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-030}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-030}
\end{equation}

Substitute into TRISO-FPT-022:

\begin{equation}
D\frac{v''}{r}
=
s\frac{v}{r}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-031}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-031}
\end{equation}

Multiply by (r):

\begin{equation}
Dv''=sv.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-032}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-032}
\end{equation}

Divide by (D):

\begin{equation}
v''=\frac{s}{D}v.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-033}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-033}
\end{equation}

Define

\begin{equation}
\lambda=\sqrt{\frac{s}{D}}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-034}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-034}
\end{equation}

Then

\begin{equation}
v''-\lambda^2v=0.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-035}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-035}
\end{equation}

A convenient general solution measured from (a) is

\begin{equation}
v(r)
=
A\sinh[\lambda(r-a)]
+
B\cosh[\lambda(r-a)].
\tag{TRISO-FPT-036}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-036}
\end{equation}

At (r=a),

\begin{equation}
v(a)=aG_b(a,s)=0.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-037}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-037}
\end{equation}

Substitute (r=a) into TRISO-FPT-036:

\begin{equation}
0=A\sinh0+B\cosh0.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-038}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-038}
\end{equation}

Use (sinh0=0) and (cosh0=1):

\begin{equation}
B=0.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-039}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-039}
\end{equation}

Thus

\begin{equation}
v(r)=A\sinh[\lambda(r-a)].
\tag{TRISO-FPT-040}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-040}
\end{equation}

At (r=b),

\begin{equation}
v(b)=bG_b(b,s)=b.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-041}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-041}
\end{equation}

Substitute TRISO-FPT-040:

\begin{equation}
b=A\sinh[\lambda(b-a)].
\tag{TRISO-FPT-042}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-042}
\end{equation}

Divide:

\begin{equation}
A
=
\frac{b}{\sinh[\lambda(b-a)]}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-043}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-043}
\end{equation}

Insert this coefficient into TRISO-FPT-040:

\begin{equation}
v(r)
=
b
\frac{\sinh[\lambda(r-a)]}
{\sinh[\lambda(b-a)]}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-044}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-044}
\end{equation}

Use (G\_b=v/r):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
G_b(r,s)
=
\frac{b}{r}
\frac{\sinh[\lambda(r-a)]}
{\sinh[\lambda(b-a)]}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FPT-045}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-045}
\end{equation}

\subsection*{22.2 Inner-exit joint transform}

Define

\begin{equation}
G_a(r,s)
=
\mathbb E_r\left[e^{-sT}\mathbf 1_{\{R_T=a\}}\right].
\tag{TRISO-FPT-046}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-046}
\end{equation}

The boundary values are

\begin{equation}
G_a(a,s)=1,
\tag{TRISO-FPT-047}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-047}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
G_a(b,s)=0.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-048}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-048}
\end{equation}

The same substitution (v=rG\_a) gives

\begin{equation}
v''-\lambda^2v=0.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-049}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-049}
\end{equation}

Choose a form that satisfies the zero condition at (b):

\begin{equation}
v(r)=C\sinh[\lambda(b-r)].
\tag{TRISO-FPT-050}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-050}
\end{equation}

At (r=a),

\begin{equation}
v(a)=aG_a(a,s)=a.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-051}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-051}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
a=C\sinh[\lambda(b-a)].
\tag{TRISO-FPT-052}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-052}
\end{equation}

Divide:

\begin{equation}
C
=
\frac{a}{\sinh[\lambda(b-a)]}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-053}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-053}
\end{equation}

Substitute:

\begin{equation}
v(r)
=
a
\frac{\sinh[\lambda(b-r)]}
{\sinh[\lambda(b-a)]}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-054}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-054}
\end{equation}

Divide by (r):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
G_a(r,s)
=
\frac{a}{r}
\frac{\sinh[\lambda(b-r)]}
{\sinh[\lambda(b-a)]}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FPT-055}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-055}
\end{equation}

\subsection*{22.3 Exit probabilities and conditional time}

Use

\begin{equation}
\sinh z\sim z
\qquad(z\rightarrow0).
\tag{TRISO-FPT-056}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-056}
\end{equation}

As (s\textbackslash\{\}rightarrow0), (lambda\textbackslash\{\}rightarrow0).

Apply TRISO-FPT-056 to TRISO-FPT-045:

\begin{equation}
G_b(r,0)
=
\frac br
\frac{\lambda(r-a)}
{\lambda(b-a)}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-057}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-057}
\end{equation}

Cancel (lambda):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
P_r(R_T=b)
=
\frac{b(r-a)}{r(b-a)}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FPT-058}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-058}
\end{equation}

Likewise,

\begin{equation}
G_a(r,0)
=
\frac ar
\frac{\lambda(b-r)}
{\lambda(b-a)}.
\tag{TRISO-FPT-059}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-059}
\end{equation}

Cancel (lambda):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
P_r(R_T=a)
=
\frac{a(b-r)}{r(b-a)}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FPT-060}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-060}
\end{equation}

The conditional outer-exit transform is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathbb E_r[e^{-sT}\mid R_T=b]
=
\frac{G_b(r,s)}{G_b(r,0)}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FPT-061}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-061}
\end{equation}

The conditional inner-exit transform is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathbb E_r[e^{-sT}\mid R_T=a]
=
\frac{G_a(r,s)}{G_a(r,0)}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FPT-062}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-062}
\end{equation}

Differentiate the outer joint transform:

\begin{equation}
\frac{\partial G_b}{\partial s}
=
\mathbb E[-Te^{-sT}\mathbf1_b].
\tag{TRISO-FPT-063}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-063}
\end{equation}

Set (s=0):

\begin{equation}
\left.\frac{\partial G_b}{\partial s}\right|_{s=0}
=
-\mathbb E[T\mathbf1_b].
\tag{TRISO-FPT-064}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-064}
\end{equation}

Hence

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathbb E[T\mathbf1_b]
=
-
\left.\frac{\partial G_b}{\partial s}\right|_{s=0}.
}
\tag{TRISO-FPT-065}\n\\label{eq:triso-fpt-065}
\end{equation}

[VERIFIED] The shell exit probabilities, conditional moments and conditional CDF were checked against analytical references and direct WOS before multilayer coupling.


\section*{23. Accelerated exact-interface renewal}

[DEFINITION] Process B replaces repeated homogeneous-region WOS wandering by exact first-passage events while retaining the frozen stochastic interface law.

[IMPORTANT] Process A stops when the distance to an interface is at most (epsilon). Process B stops at the physical interface. The two stopping times are not identical.

\subsection*{23.1 Initial finite-capture mass}

For uniform births in an inner sphere of radius (a), the total sphere volume is

\begin{equation}
V_a=\frac{4\pi}{3}a^3.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-020}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-020}
\end{equation}

The volume inside radius (a-\textbackslash\{\}epsilon) is

\begin{equation}
V_{a-\epsilon}
=
\frac{4\pi}{3}(a-\epsilon)^3.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-021}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-021}
\end{equation}

The capture-shell volume is

\begin{equation}
V_{\rm cap}
=
V_a-V_{a-\epsilon}.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-022}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-022}
\end{equation}

The capture-shell probability is

\begin{equation}
P_{\rm cap}
=
\frac{V_{\rm cap}}{V_a}.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-023}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-023}
\end{equation}

Substitute TRISO-WOS-020 through TRISO-WOS-022:

\begin{equation}
P_{\rm cap}
=
\frac{a^3-(a-\epsilon)^3}{a^3}.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-024}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-024}
\end{equation}

Divide by (a\textasciicircum{}3):

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
P_{\rm cap}
=
1-\left(1-\frac{\epsilon}{a}\right)^3.
}
\tag{TRISO-WOS-025}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-025}
\end{equation}

For (a=50\textbackslash\{\},\textbackslash\{\}mu\textbackslash\{\}mathrm m) and (epsilon=0.1\textbackslash\{\},\textbackslash\{\}mu\textbackslash\{\}mathrm m),

\begin{equation}
\frac{\epsilon}{a}=0.002.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-026}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-026}
\end{equation}

Therefore

\begin{equation}
P_{\rm cap}=1-(0.998)^3.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-027}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-027}
\end{equation}

Numerically,

\begin{equation}
\boxed{P_{\rm cap}=0.005988008.}
\tag{TRISO-WOS-028}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-028}
\end{equation}

[EMPIRICAL COMPATIBILITY] The executed Process-A/Process-B controlled discrepancy is small at the declared finite-(epsilon) statistical precision, but it is not an identity.

\subsection*{23.2 Two-layer accelerated verification}

[VERIFIED] The corrected two-layer accelerator uses the exact centred-ball kernel in the inner region and the exact shell kernel in the outer region.

The executed accelerated/FV RMS CDF difference is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\mathrm{RMS}(F_B-F_{FV})
=
0.002895.
}
\tag{TRISO-VER-200}\n\\label{eq:triso-ver-200}
\end{equation}

The maximum absolute difference is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\max_t|F_B-F_{FV}|
=
0.005215.
}
\tag{TRISO-VER-201}\n\\label{eq:triso-ver-201}
\end{equation}

The direct 100-nm WOS benchmark required approximately (1207.2) production steps per history.

The accelerated benchmark required approximately (139.04) renewal events per history.

The event-count ratio is

\begin{equation}
\frac{1207.2}{139.04}
=
8.68.
\tag{TRISO-VER-202}\n\\label{eq:triso-ver-202}
\end{equation}

Thus the controlled event-count reduction is approximately

\begin{equation}
\boxed{8.68\times.}
\tag{TRISO-VER-203}\n\\label{eq:triso-ver-203}
\end{equation}

\section*{24. Five-layer computational pathology}

[VERIFIED] Direct production WOS produced 8/8 censored histories at (10\textasciicircum{}6) steps per history in the targeted five-layer diagnostic.

[VERIFIED] The exact-shell accelerated five-layer diagnostic produced 0/16 releases and 16/16 capped histories at 100000 renewals per history.

The renewal counts by layer were

\begin{equation}
(N_K,N_B,N_I,N_S,N_O)
=
(24,1599974,2,0,0).
\tag{TRISO-WOS-030}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-030}
\end{equation}

The total number of renewals was

\begin{equation}
N_{\rm tot}=1600000.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-031}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-031}
\end{equation}

The Buffer renewal fraction is

\begin{equation}
f_B
=
\frac{1599974}{1600000}.
\tag{TRISO-WOS-032}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-032}
\end{equation}

Numerically,

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
f_B=0.99998375.
}
\tag{TRISO-WOS-033}\n\\label{eq:triso-wos-033}
\end{equation}

[VERIFIED] Removing homogeneous-region wandering did not remove the computational pathology.

[INFERRED] The dominant remaining cost is repeated rare interface-state recurrence.

\section*{25. Interface-state Markov-renewal reduction}

\subsection*{25.1 Eight transient states}

There are four physical internal interfaces.

Each interface has two post-interface material sides.

Therefore

\begin{equation}
N_{\rm states}
=
4\times2.
\tag{TRISO-MR-001}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-001}
\end{equation}

Hence

\begin{equation}
\boxed{N_{\rm states}=8.}
\tag{TRISO-MR-002}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-002}
\end{equation}

Define

\begin{equation}
S_0=\text{Kernel side of Kernel/Buffer}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-003}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-003}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
S_1=\text{Buffer side of Kernel/Buffer}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-004}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-004}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
S_2=\text{Buffer side of Buffer/IPyC}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-005}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-005}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
S_3=\text{IPyC side of Buffer/IPyC}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-006}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-006}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
S_4=\text{IPyC side of IPyC/SiC}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-007}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-007}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
S_5=\text{SiC side of IPyC/SiC}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-008}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-008}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
S_6=\text{SiC side of SiC/OPyC}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-009}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-009}
\end{equation}

\begin{equation}
S_7=\text{OPyC side of SiC/OPyC}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-010}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-010}
\end{equation}

The exterior release state is absorbing.

\subsection*{25.2 First-step equation from the Kernel-side state}

Define

\begin{equation}
\Phi_i(s)
=
\mathbb E_i[e^{-sT_{\rm rel}}].
\tag{TRISO-MR-011}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-011}
\end{equation}

From (S\_0), the exact centred-ball transform to the Kernel/Buffer interface is (H\_K(s)).

At that interface, reflection returns to (S\_0).

Transmission enters (S\_1).

Thus

\begin{equation}
\Phi_0
=
H_Kp_{K\rightarrow K}\Phi_0
+
H_Kp_{K\rightarrow B}\Phi_1.
\tag{TRISO-MR-012}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-012}
\end{equation}

Identify

\begin{equation}
K_{00}
=
H_Kp_{K\rightarrow K}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-013}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-013}
\end{equation}

Identify

\begin{equation}
K_{01}
=
H_Kp_{K\rightarrow B}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-014}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-014}
\end{equation}

All other row-0 entries are zero.

\subsection*{25.3 First-step equation from Buffer side of the inner interface}

From (S\_1), the Buffer shell can first exit inward with transform (G\_B\textasciicircum{}-).

It can first exit outward with transform (G\_B\textasciicircum{}+).

If it exits inward, transmission enters Kernel state (S\_0).

If it exits inward and reflects, it returns to Buffer state (S\_1).

If it exits outward and reflects, it moves to Buffer state (S\_2).

If it exits outward and transmits, it enters IPyC state (S\_3).

Therefore

\begin{equation}
\Phi_1
=
G_B^-p_{B\rightarrow K}\Phi_0
+
G_B^-p_{B\rightarrow B}^{(I_0)}\Phi_1
+
G_B^+p_{B\rightarrow B}^{(I_1)}\Phi_2
+
G_B^+p_{B\rightarrow I}\Phi_3.
\tag{TRISO-MR-015}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-015}
\end{equation}

The first coefficient is

\begin{equation}
K_{10}
=
G_B^-p_{B\rightarrow K}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-016}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-016}
\end{equation}

The second is

\begin{equation}
K_{11}
=
G_B^-p_{B\rightarrow B}^{(I_0)}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-017}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-017}
\end{equation}

The third is

\begin{equation}
K_{12}
=
G_B^+p_{B\rightarrow B}^{(I_1)}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-018}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-018}
\end{equation}

The fourth is

\begin{equation}
K_{13}
=
G_B^+p_{B\rightarrow I}.
\tag{TRISO-MR-019}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-019}
\end{equation}

The (S\_2) row has the same four possible destination states, but (G\_B\textasciicircum{}pm) are evaluated at the reinsertion radius adjacent to the outer Buffer interface.

\subsection*{25.4 IPyC and SiC state rows}

For (S\_3) and (S\_4), use the IPyC shell transforms (G\_I\textasciicircum{}-) and (G\_I\textasciicircum{}+).

The inward interface is Buffer/IPyC.

The outward interface is IPyC/SiC.

The same first-step construction gives four probability-weighted transform terms in each row.

For (S\_5) and (S\_6), use the SiC shell transforms (G\_S\textasciicircum{}-) and (G\_S\textasciicircum{}+).

The inward interface is IPyC/SiC.

The outward interface is SiC/OPyC.

Again, each row contains the two exit sides multiplied by the two interface outcomes.

\subsection*{25.5 OPyC state and direct release}

From (S\_7), inner shell exit reaches the SiC/OPyC interface.

Outer shell exit reaches the absorbing particle exterior.

The direct release transform is

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
B_7(s)=G_O^+(s).
}
\tag{TRISO-MR-020}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-020}
\end{equation}

For every other transient state,

\begin{equation}
B_i(s)=0,
\qquad i\ne7.
\tag{TRISO-MR-021}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-021}
\end{equation}

\subsection*{25.6 Matrix rearrangement without skipped algebra}

Collect the eight first-step equations:

\begin{equation}
\boldsymbol\Phi
=
\mathbf K\boldsymbol\Phi
+
\mathbf B.
\tag{TRISO-MR-022}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-022}
\end{equation}

Subtract (mathbf K\textbackslash\{\}boldsymbol\textbackslash\{\}Phi) from both sides:

\begin{equation}
\boldsymbol\Phi
-
\mathbf K\boldsymbol\Phi
=
\mathbf B.
\tag{TRISO-MR-023}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-023}
\end{equation}

Write (oldsymbol\textbackslash\{\}Phi=\textbackslash\{\}mathbf I\textbackslash\{\}boldsymbol\textbackslash\{\}Phi):

\begin{equation}
\mathbf I\boldsymbol\Phi
-
\mathbf K\boldsymbol\Phi
=
\mathbf B.
\tag{TRISO-MR-024}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-024}
\end{equation}

Factor (oldsymbol\textbackslash\{\}Phi):

\begin{equation}
(\mathbf I-\mathbf K)\boldsymbol\Phi
=
\mathbf B.
\tag{TRISO-MR-025}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-025}
\end{equation}

When (mathbf I-\textbackslash\{\}mathbf K) is nonsingular, left-multiply by its inverse:

\begin{equation}
(\mathbf I-\mathbf K)^{-1}
(\mathbf I-\mathbf K)
\boldsymbol\Phi
=
(\mathbf I-\mathbf K)^{-1}\mathbf B.
\tag{TRISO-MR-026}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-026}
\end{equation}

Use the inverse identity:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\boldsymbol\Phi
=
(\mathbf I-\mathbf K)^{-1}\mathbf B.
}
\tag{TRISO-MR-027}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-027}
\end{equation}

\subsection*{25.7 Neumann-series path interpretation}

If

\begin{equation}
\rho(\mathbf K)<1,
\tag{TRISO-MR-028}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-028}
\end{equation}

then

\begin{equation}
(\mathbf I-\mathbf K)^{-1}
=
\mathbf I
+
\mathbf K
+
\mathbf K^2
+
\mathbf K^3
+\cdots.
\tag{TRISO-MR-029}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-029}
\end{equation}

Multiply by (mathbf B):

\begin{equation}
\boldsymbol\Phi
=
\mathbf B
+
\mathbf K\mathbf B
+
\mathbf K^2\mathbf B
+
\mathbf K^3\mathbf B
+\cdots.
\tag{TRISO-MR-030}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-030}
\end{equation}

The term (mathbf B) is direct absorption without another transient renewal.

The term (mathbf K\textbackslash\{\}mathbf B) is absorption after one transient renewal.

The term (mathbf K\textasciicircum{}2\textbackslash\{\}mathbf B) is absorption after two transient renewals.

Thus the inverse sums arbitrarily long repeated interface-renewal paths without sampling each path individually.

\subsection*{25.8 Uniform-volume initial source}

The kernel radial probability density is

\begin{equation}
f_R(r)
=
\frac{3r^2}{R_1^3},
\qquad
0\le r\le R_1.
\tag{TRISO-MR-031}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-031}
\end{equation}

The exact kernel first-exit transform from radius (r) is (H\_K(r,s)).

After reaching Kernel/Buffer, reflection gives (S\_0) and transmission gives (S\_1).

Define

\begin{equation}
Q(s)
=
p_{K\rightarrow K}\Phi_0(s)
+
p_{K\rightarrow B}\Phi_1(s).
\tag{TRISO-MR-032}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-032}
\end{equation}

Average over the birth distribution:

\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\Phi_{\rm init}(s)
=
\int_0^{R_1}
\frac{3r^2}{R_1^3}
H_K(r,s)
Q(s)\,dr.
}
\tag{TRISO-MR-033}\n\\label{eq:triso-mr-033}
\end{equation}

\subsection*{25.9 Process A, B and C distinction}

[DEFINITION] Process A is finite-capture production WOS.

[DEFINITION] Process B is explicit accelerated exact-interface renewal.

[DEFINITION] Process C is the deterministic matrix reduction of Process B.

[EXACT TARGET] The mathematical reduction target is (B=C).

[EMPIRICAL COMPATIBILITY] Process A and Process B/C have controlled finite-(epsilon) compatibility evidence.

[IMPORTANT] Neither statement is a proof of the (epsilon\textbackslash\{\}rightarrow0) continuum limit.
